Bravida · BRAV-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 19 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Bravida memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Bravida was written: 49,144 words, against 10,096 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 23,769 words · written 19 October 2026, 10:19 to 10:38 UK, Opus, ricardinho login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Bravida (BRAV-SE): first set of questions, the foundational components of the investment case

Written 2026-10-19 for the Investment Analyst answering the second set (the operating and financial model, 18 months to four years).

Before the answers: data checks, bases and modelling anchors

Data checks.

  • The latest Earnings Review covers Q2 2026 (three months to 30 June 2026, reported 13 July 2026). This matches the last period in the FactSet block (06/2026), so it can be relied on. The next results are Q3 2026 on 23 October 2026. Instalco reports at about the same time and is the read-across.
  • Vintage of the research:
  • Before the Q2 print: the Risks and Business Foundations digests (2 June 2026), Leadership Foundations (10 June) and the Key Question report (19 June).
  • After it: the Change Forces report (17 September), the sector digests (13 October) and the dashboard (16 to 18 October).
  • The Q1 2026 Earnings Review file cites some sources dated 20 to 21 October 2026, which is after today: the completion of the second buyback and Veidekke's autumn market update. Treat those as unverified until re-read.
  • Price authority: the dashboard, 149.0 SEK (16 October 2026). On FactSet the market capitalisation is about SEK 30.8bn and the enterprise value SEK 32.7bn (including leases and pensions).

Bases that will mislead the model if mixed.

  1. Three different EBITA lines measure Q2 2026: - the company's adjusted EBITA excludes the SEK 158m ABEKA gain and the SEK 40m restructuring charge (SEK 452m); - FactSet's "EBIT (adjusted)" of SEK 413m is reported EBITA less the gain, so the restructuring is still inside it; - consensus EBIT is each broker's own definition, and some brokers include the ABEKA gain in FY2026.
  2. FY2026 consensus EPS of SEK 7.20 is not clean. - For some brokers it contains the ABEKA gain, about SEK 0.77 a share and probably tax-free. For some it contains about SEK 70 to 80m of Swedish restructuring. - Clean FY2026 EPS is about SEK 6.5 to 6.9, and the clean FY2026 consensus EBITA margin is about 6.3%. - So consensus FY2026 EPS growth of +22% is flattered, and FY2027 growth on a clean base is higher than the +22% printed.
  3. The consensus P&L lines do not reconcile with each other. - Net income of SEK 1,548m does not match EPS of 7.20 × about 204m shares, which gives about SEK 1,470m. The broker-sheet median net income is SEK 1,468m. - EBIT less pre-tax profit implies a net finance cost of only SEK 60 to 67m a year, against SEK 181m in FY2025 and SEK 197 to 209m in FY2023 to 2024 (FactSet's figure includes lease interest). - Build the bridge from consensus EBIT and EPS, and model net interest and tax yourself.
  4. Leverage has three readings: 1.3x on the company's basis (Q2 2026); 1.68x on FactSet's, which includes leases; about 0.6 to 0.7x excluding IFRS 16.
  5. Cash conversion has two definitions. - The company's "cash conversion" is operating cash flow over EBITA, last twelve months: 77% at Q2 2026. - FactSet's FCF over net income was 43% in FY2025. FactSet's FCF line seems to deduct lease repayments: operating cash flow of 1,453 less capex of 156 is 1,297, against a FactSet FCF of 530. This is a hypothesis; verify before using either series.
  6. The dividend consensus (SEK 4.00, 4.25, 4.40 for FY2026 to FY2028) implies payouts of 56%, 48% and 47%. The policy is at least 50% and the actual FY2025 payout was 63%, so these DPS lines look stale.

Anchors.

  • FY2025:
  • revenue SEK 28,208m, -4.9% (organic -5%, FX -2%, M&A +2%);
  • adjusted EBITA about SEK 1,668 to 1,689m, a margin of 5.9 to 6.0%;
  • EPS SEK 5.88 on the brokers' basis; DPS SEK 3.80.
  • Last twelve months to Q2 2026: adjusted EBITA SEK 1,801m on revenue of SEK 29,020m, a margin of 6.2%.
  • Backlog at Q2 2026: SEK 20,372m (+21%), 1.34x last-twelve-month installation sales.
  • Consensus (FactSet median, five to six brokers):
  • revenue SEK 30,390m, 33,889m and 35,125m for FY2026 to FY2028, and SEK 33,435m for FY2029 (one broker);
  • EBIT SEK 2,002m, 2,370m and 2,507m (margins 6.6%, 7.0%, 7.1%);
  • EPS SEK 7.20, 8.78 and 9.45.

External change forces

C6510 questions
C-0170C-0252C-0355C-0463C-0533C-0672C-0748C-0874C-0952C-1066

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judgement. The external forces give Bravida the strongest volume set-up it has had since 2022. Two legs act at once:

  • a cyclical Nordic construction recovery, still feeding revenue through its lag into 2027;
  • a contracted, structural data-centre leg that lifts the business from 1% to 3% of revenue in data centres to about 8% in 2027.

These justify revenue growth well above the 2.4% a year of FY2022 to FY2025.

Four conditions the second-set model must respect.

  1. Visibility. The H1 2026 "inflection" is largely a return to 2024 volumes. True growth above prior peak only starts showing from H2 2026.
  2. The cycle's rate of change. The cyclical tailwind is past its best: the Riksbank and Norges Bank are hiking again, and Swedish construction growth is expected to slow from about 9% (2026) to about 4% (2027). Volumes in 2028 to 2029 are therefore vulnerable just as the data-centre contracts roll off. That leaves a revenue air-pocket in 2028 to 2029 unless the data-centre pipeline refills; consensus shows it as FY2028 +3.6% and FY2029 -4.8% (one broker).
  3. Margin. The external forces that drive volume do not drive margin. Wages (+8% Swedish minimums in 2027), copper, equipment lead times and 80% subcontracting on the growth contracts all sit between the extra revenue and extra profit.
  4. Concentration. The structural leg depends on three externally financed co-location developers, in the one theme whose financing has just started to crack.

The external picture in one line. Strongly positive for 2026 to 2027 volume, neutral for margin, fragile for 2028 to 2029.

C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility?

C70

Question asked To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

Judgement. Demand is changing on two separate legs. The model must keep them apart, because they have different visibility, different margins and different ends.

Leg 1: a cyclical Nordic construction recovery.

  • It started in Q1 2026 from a two-year trough and is now broad-based.
  • Visibility is about 12 to 18 months:
  • the order book is about eight months of revenue;
  • installation revenue lags construction starts by two to four quarters;
  • the rate cycle that drove the turn is now reversing.

Leg 2: a structural layer.

  • Nordic data centres, defence and public infrastructure.
  • Only the data-centre part is contracted with visibility beyond two years, and it is concentrated in three contracts that end between 2028 and 2029.

Evidence that the cyclical leg is real.

  • Organic growth went -7.7% (Q2 2025), -2.5%, -2.2%, +0.9%, then +9.0% (Q2 2026).
  • In Q2 2026 every country grew: Finland +18%, Denmark +11%, Sweden +8%, Norway +5%.
  • Order intake rose 44% to SEK 11,691m and the backlog 21% to SEK 20,372m. Q1 Swedish order intake was +16%.
  • Outside the company:
  • Swedish building starts +8.0% (July 2026), non-residential +13.2%;
  • Swedish construction order books are back above normal (DNB Carnegie, 29 September);
  • Instalco grew +8.4% organically after six negative quarters.

What the headline overstates. The +9% is mostly a base effect.

  • On a two-year stacked basis, organic growth was about +0.6% in Q2 2026 (0.923 × 1.090) and about -4% in Q1.
  • Q2 2026 revenue (SEK 7,629m) is still below Q2 2024 (SEK 7,694m), and Q1 2026 (SEK 7,045m) is below Q1 2024 (SEK 7,275m). These figures include currency drag and the M&A and ABEKA perimeter effects.
  • Volume has returned to the 2024 run-rate; it has not exceeded it.
  • Comparisons stay easy through Q4 2026 (Q3 2025 -2.5%, Q4 2025 -2.2%) and become hard from Q2 2027.

The structural leg.

  • Over SEK 7bn won in two months:
  • Green Mountain, SEK 4.3bn to 2029, delivered through a joint venture with Backe Industri. Confirm whether SEK 4.3bn is Bravida's share or the joint venture's total.
  • atNorth, about EUR 200m over about two years, as principal contractor including civil works.
  • EcoDataCenter, SEK 650m, to Q1 2028.
  • At the 2027 peak this is roughly SEK 2.6 to 2.9bn of revenue a year, about 8% of FY2027 consensus revenue, against 1% to 3% of revenue before Q2 2026.
  • Consensus already carries it:
  • its geographic split has Finland +31% and Norway +21% in FY2027;
  • the single FY2029 estimate falls 4.8%, which is the contract roll-off.

The cyclical tailwind's rate of change is peaking.

  • The Riksbank has signalled hikes: Nordea expects +25bp in November 2026, and Deutsche Bank expects 2.50% by mid-2027 from 1.75%.
  • Norges Bank hiked to 4.50% in September 2026.
  • Veidekke (vintage unverified) has Swedish construction production at +9% in 2026 and +4% in 2027.
  • Because revenue lags starts, the 2026 start recovery still feeds 2027 revenue. Higher rates in 2027 would show in 2028 to 2029 volumes.

Mix shields some of this. New residential is only 7% to 10% of sales, public clients 21% and service 48%. Service held up in the downturn (+8% in 2024 while installation fell 3%).

Implication for the model.

  • Model core (ex data-centre) organic growth separately: about +6% to +8% in H2 2026 on easy comparisons, +3% to +5% in 2027, +2% to +4% in 2028.
  • Add the data-centre leg on top: about +5pp to FY2027 growth, about zero to FY2028, and negative in FY2029 unless refilled.
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C52

Judgement. Intensity is easing at the top of the market and unchanged to intense in the tail. The net direction over the last six months is modestly favourable. That rests on two conditions: recovering volume filling the tail's idle capacity, and the three largest players staying financially constrained.

The top.

  • The "Big-3" (Bravida, Instalco, Assemblin Caverion Group, ACG) prioritise margin over volume (ABG Sundal Collier).
  • Instalco is under a founder's "margin, margin, margin" mandate, with Swedish margin up 40bp to 7.0% in Q2 2026. It carries 2.6x leverage, so it cannot buy share.
  • ACG is a Triton-owned company rated B by Fitch and focused on integration.

The tail.

  • More than 25,000 small firms still submit zero-margin bids in traditional construction.
  • Swedish tenders draw about 15 bidders against a historical 5 (Instalco research, October 2026).
  • Peab is described as volume-first.
  • New-build contractors are moving into renovation: Balco's renovation margin is 2.3%, and 83% of exterior-work operators report margin compression.
  • Southern Sweden is still under price pressure (Head of Sweden; Deutsche Bank).

New fronts.

  • In data centres, ACG set up a Swedish data-centre business area on 31 August 2026. It and Bravida are named as the only Nordic players with the white-collar depth (DNB Carnegie). The niche Bravida's growth relies on now has a direct, larger competitor.
  • In acquisitions:
  • InstallatørGruppen (private equity) has made 48 deals since 2023;
  • Nimlas has 140 companies and SEK 10bn of revenue, and made SEK 1.5bn of acquisitions in 2025.
  • Both bid against Bravida's 5 to 6x EBITA bolt-ons.

The test in the next six months.

  • Swedish minimum wages are expected to rise 8% in 2027 (SEB).
  • If competitors pass that on in 2027 tenders, the industry is rational. If the tail absorbs it to keep crews busy, Bravida's traditional book stays squeezed.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C55

Judgement. No disruption force is strengthening against the core business: physical electrical, heating, plumbing and ventilation work in buildings, plus service. The three forces that are strengthening act at the data-centre top end, which is where Bravida's growth now sits, and on balance they act against the installer's share of value there:

  • operators procuring power and cooling equipment directly from manufacturers to beat three-year lead times;
  • prefabricated modular power and cooling skids that cut on-site labour (prefabrication can raise on-site productivity by up to 40%, Jefferies);
  • standardisation of hyperscaler packages, which commoditises installers.

The offset in the company's favour. The sector research (9 October 2026) finds that contractor pricing power in data centres lies in complex co-locator installations and grid work, not in large standardised hyperscaler packages.

  • Bravida's three new customers (Green Mountain, atNorth, EcoDataCenter) are Nordic co-locators, the favourable shape.
  • EcoDataCenter is an eight-time repeat customer.
  • The company's answer to disintermediation is its Specialist Package Contractor model.

In the core, digitalisation is a mild positive.

  • Building automation is about SEK 1bn of sales.
  • The Elpalko acquisition and 5D BIM support it.
  • The EU sub-metering mandate (remotely readable devices by January 2027) adds content per building.

Being disproved: the idea that software or AI disintermediates physical installation.

Not disruption, but a risk transfer. The atNorth role includes civil works as principal contractor. It should be modelled as a risk change, not as a disruption.

C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C63

Judgement. Political change is supportive of demand. Its timing is slower than the headline sums imply, and the regulatory cost is the procurement scrutiny the company brought on itself.

What is funded.

  • Sweden: defence appropriations of SEK 175bn in 2026 (+SEK 27bn); the transport plan of SEK 1,171bn for 2026 to 2037 (+SEK 372bn in real terms); an expansionary SEK 80bn budget aimed at real wages and housing starts.
  • Finland: EUR 2.8bn for transport (2025 to 2032), plus NATO-related defence building.
  • Norway: NOK 102.1bn for transport, much of it absorbed by 5% road-cost inflation.

The renovation law (EPBD).

  • Sweden transposed the recast directive on 1 October 2026, with the national renovation plan due by the end of 2026.
  • No member state met the May 2026 deadline, and infringement proceedings were opened against all 27 on 15 July 2026.
  • The sector's own read is right: the law lengthens the tail of renovation demand; it does not drive the next year.
  • In Norway (19% of sales), the coalition collapsed over adopting EU energy directives, so the EPBD tailwind there is uncertain.

The cost side.

  • Municipal clients audit Bravida more aggressively since Region Skåne. Public clients are 21% of sales.
  • CSRD reporting adds compliance cost.
  • Public roll-outs are "highly delayed" by planning and permitting (Deutsche Bank; Instalco).

Implication for the model. Treat public and defence demand as a stabiliser that supports 2027 to 2029 volumes, not as a 2026 to 2027 accelerator.

C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

❌ D33

Judgement. The net effect is a headwind, and it intensifies into 2027.

  • It lands on fixed-price installation work with a 9 to 12 month lag.
  • It bites hardest on the new data-centre contracts, which are heavy in copper and electrical equipment.
  • Most of the risk sits in Sweden (47% of sales), whose contracts lack automatic price indexation.

Labour is the largest item.

  • Labour, subcontractors and materials make up about 84% of revenue as production cost.
  • SEB expects Swedish agreed minimum wages to rise 8% for 2027, against agreements of about 3.2% a year before.
  • Unless repriced, that change alone is the biggest margin risk to FY2027 to FY2028.
  • Skilled electricians and commissioning engineers are scarce across the Nordics.

Materials.

  • Copper: LME cash price forecast +38.4% in 2026 to about USD 13,770/t (EIU). Data centres use 27kt of copper per GW, 75% of it in power distribution (Morgan Stanley).
  • Equipment lead times: about three years for switchgear and transformers.
  • Suppliers are passing on cost: Lindab's margin was squeezed and Systemair raised prices; insulation makers raised prices by 31% to 37% (Kingspan) and 35% (Saint-Gobain).
  • Construction cost inflation: 3.7% in Sweden and 5.4% in Norway (September 2026).

Concentration.

  • A few wholesalers dominate: Ahlsell has about 30% of Swedish installation distribution, and four groups hold 82% of Swedish electrical distribution.
  • Bravida's procurement team is 50 to 60 people. An industry expert places it in the bottom 5% of peers for bargaining power.
  • The subcontractor tier is fragile: 841 construction bankruptcy applications in Finland in 2024, and repeated Danish construction failures. On the data-centre jobs Bravida relies on 80% subcontracted labour.

Mitigants.

  • Automatic indexation on Danish jobs longer than 12 months.
  • Cost-plus pricing on complex work.
  • Index clauses that adjust with a lag.
  • Central frame agreements, and early material-purchase agreements signed by branches.

Sensitivity. 0.5pp of gross margin is about SEK 150 to 170m of EBITA, or about 7% of EPS. Input costs are the most likely source of a margin miss against consensus in FY2027.

C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

C72

Judgement. Bravida's own cycle turned in Q1 2026 and accelerated in Q2. The core is in the early-to-mid upswing, and a contracted data-centre ramp starts on top of it from H2 2026.

  • Reported growth will look strongest over the next four to six quarters: easy comparisons plus the data-centre ramp.
  • The rate of change of the core probably peaks around H1 2027, when comparisons harden and the 2026 rate reversal starts to feed through with its lag.

Evidence for the turn.

  • Reported revenue growth by quarter: -9.4% (Q2 2025), -2.2%, -2.4%, +2.3%, +9.4% (Q2 2026).
  • Order intake: +5% in Q1 2026, then +44% in Q2, including Green Mountain.
  • The backlog-to-installation-sales ratio rose from 1.13x to 1.34x in one quarter.
  • Q3 2026 intake will include atNorth (about SEK 2.2bn) and EcoDataCenter (SEK 650m), so the Q3 backlog should rise again, plausibly above SEK 22bn.
  • The margin cycle has also turned. The adjusted EBITA margin went from a 2024 trough of 5.2% to 6.0% (FY2025) and 6.2% (last twelve months to Q2 2026).

Where it is still a rebound. Volume in H1 2026 equalled the 2024 level; it did not exceed it (see C-01).

What to model.

  • An installer is late-cycle: revenue follows consultants' and architects' work by 6 to 12 months and construction starts by several quarters.
  • The 2026 recovery in Swedish starts therefore supports 2027 revenue.
  • Rate hikes from November 2026 would weigh on 2028 to 2029 starts and revenue, just as the data-centre contracts roll off.
  • That makes 2028 to 2029 the vulnerable years in the cycle.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

❌ D48

Judgement. Macro forces act on Bravida more than on its sector average, for three company-specific reasons:

  • it is a late-cycle, rate-geared installer in Sweden, which Goldman Sachs ranks among the most rate-sensitive developed economies because mortgages reset;
  • 53% of sales is in NOK, DKK and EUR, with translation exposure left 0% hedged;
  • 100% of its debt is floating-rate and all of it is in SEK.

Where it bites hardest. At the company level, through currency and the Swedish rate path. At the sector level, through construction volumes. At the industry level, through the discount rate (US 10-year about 5.1%; a regime that is refusing re-ratings), which bears on the multiple, not on earnings.

The direction has turned.

  • It was a tailwind while the Riksbank cut from 4.00% to 1.75%.
  • It is now neutral to negative: the Riksbank has signalled hikes, a November hike is "increasingly likely" (Barclays; Nordea), and Norges Bank is at 4.50% after a September hike.
  • The firm's Fitness for Fighting digest moved Sweden from Overweight to Neutral this month.
  • Partial offsets: Swedish consumer confidence is above normal for the first time since 2021 (DNB Carnegie, 29 September), and the fiscal stance is expansionary.

Currency sensitivities.

  • A 10% stronger SEK against NOK and DKK takes about SEK 0.28 off EPS, about 3% of FY2027 consensus EPS.
  • A 10% DKK move alone is worth SEK 0.14.
  • Consensus expects a stronger krona (EUR/SEK 10.20 to 10.50).
  • Translation took 2% off sales in 2025 and in Q1 2026.

Interest sensitivity. 100bp on about SEK 2.5 to 3bn of floating net debt (excluding leases) is about SEK 25 to 30m pre-tax, about 1% to 1.5% of EPS.

For the model. Assume a 1% to 2% annual translation drag in 2026 to 2027 unless the forward curve says otherwise, and a flat-to-higher funding cost from 2027.

C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C74

Judgement. The Nordic data-centre build-out (cheap clean power, cool climate, grid access) is the sector driver accelerating Bravida's demand most, with defence second.

The step change.

  • Data centres were 1% to 3% of revenue before Q2 2026.
  • Over SEK 7bn of contracts were won in two months, making data centres about 8% of revenue at the 2027 peak.
  • The capability is real: about 200MW installed since 2009, a central special-projects office of about 70 people, and 40 to 60 white-collar managers per site.
  • DNB Carnegie argues data centres could be 20% to 30% of EBITA by 2030. That requires repeated refills; it is not evidenced.

How much it accelerates growth. Data centres add about 5pp to FY2027 revenue growth, and their share of the backlog jumps to about 30%: SEK 7.15bn of SEK 23.2bn after the Q3 additions, ignoring burn.

The overlooked fragility: the customers' financing.

  • Bravida's customers are not hyperscalers with balance sheets. They are externally financed co-location developers, owned by real-estate and private-equity groups: Green Mountain by Azrieli, atNorth by Partners Group, EcoDataCenter by Areim (ownership to be verified).
  • This week's environment digest records the first credit crack inside the AI build-out's financing (CoreWeave bonds below 78 cents). The Wisdom Library pattern "a capital-intensive theme cracks at its financing before its demand" applies to this customer type.
  • The risk is a delay, renegotiation or payment failure on a developer whose funding tightens, not a fall in end demand.
  • The Northvolt receivable loss (about SEK 100m) is the company's own precedent for counterparty failure in a hyped Nordic build-out.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

C52

Judgement. Four further dynamics matter: one positive and near-dated, three negative or two-sided.

  1. The legal receivables (positive near-term, with a tail). - Rogaland District Court (June 2026) awarded about NOK 330m plus NOK 150m of interest and costs on New Stavanger Hospital, about SEK 465m. That is roughly SEK 2.3 a share and about 1.5% of market capitalisation. - The cash should arrive in H2 2026 unless the client appeals; the appeal status is unknown. - The Danish public-sector dispute (SEK 800m) is in arbitration until 2028. - Together these were the SEK 1.2bn of "trapped" receivables, about a year of operating cash flow.
  2. Skilled labour scarcity (two-sided). - It supports rational pricing among the largest players on complex work. - It caps the volume Bravida can deliver with its own staff, which is why it uses 80% subcontractors on mega-projects. Headcount was cut 4% to about 13,400 in the trough.
  3. The AI-capex and financing cycle (negative tail). Bravida now trades as an AI-infrastructure beneficiary (see C-08 and C-82).
  4. Weather and seasonality (timing). Q1 is structurally the weakest quarter (4.5% to 4.9% margin), and a cold Q1 2026 hit facade and outdoor peers. Q4 carries the year: 8.1% to 8.4% margin and the largest cash inflow.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds, and what is the overall external change picture?

C66

Question asked Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

Judgement. External tailwinds are strong for volume in 2026 to 2027. External headwinds are moderate and concentrated on margin. The overall picture is favourable for revenue, neutral for margin and turning less favourable for 2028 to 2029.

The sector's themes, one by one (Sector digest, 13 October 2026).

  • "Volume returning to a pruned cost base drives earnings, not price; revenue lags construction starts by several quarters."
  • Bravida fits this exactly: headcount down 4%, Sweden merged, a SEK 65m saving.
  • Earnings upgrades should land in 2027 to 2028 rather than now. This is a tailwind.
  • "The skilled technician is both the pricing power and the volume cap; a subcontractor-heavy cost base leaks the scarcity margin to the sub-tier."
  • On the data-centre contracts Bravida runs 20% own staff and 80% subcontractors, so it leaks the scarcity margin by design. This is a headwind to the margin on the growth leg.
  • "A renovation law in infringement lengthens the tail; it does not drive next year." The EPBD is neutral for 2027 and supportive later.
  • "Swedish minimum wages rising 8% for 2027 cap price." A headwind.

The industry's themes (Industry digest).

  • "In the data-centre wave the makers are paid, the channel and the crew are not." Bravida is the crew. It earns about 6% EBITA on the theme, while the equipment makers upstream earn about 20%.
  • "Pay for conversion once the orders are in." Supportive from 2026 to 2028.
  • "Where someone else owns the crew, the installer takes the profit." On the subcontracted data-centre work someone else does own part of the crew.

The value chain, all together.

  • Tailwinds:
  • demand (data centres, defence, transport, renovation);
  • the rate-driven construction turn, which is still feeding through its lag;
  • competitive discipline at the top.
  • Headwinds:
  • wages, copper and three-year equipment lead times on fixed-price work;
  • concentrated wholesalers;
  • a tail that dumps price if the recovery stalls;
  • currency translation;
  • a rate cycle turning up.

Strength, roughly.

  • Tailwinds add about 7% to 9% a year to revenue in 2026 to 2027.
  • Headwinds could take 30 to 60bp off the margin path consensus assumes, unless pricing passes them on.

Internal change forces

C5513 questions
C-1158C-1258C-1335C-1460C-1550C-1655C-1732C-1848C-1964C-2060C-8638C-8750C-2156

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judgement. The company has cleaned its base:

  • Denmark is fixed;
  • Sweden is merged;
  • ABEKA is sold;
  • a new CFO and new Heads of Sweden and M&A are in post;
  • buybacks have begun;
  • risk controls have been added after Region Skåne.

It has done this without changing its strategy, its chief executive, its targets or its operating philosophy.

The internal story is now one of exposure, not improvement.

  • The one genuinely new internal choice is to take on principal-contractor data-centre work. That raises growth and raises risk, and is resourced centrally rather than through the branches.
  • The negative internal developments (lower cash conversion, a higher project-risk profile, thin internal audit, culture and safety metrics below target, EBITA-only incentives) are the costs of that choice. They are present now; the benefits arrive in 2027.

For the model.

  • Internal forces add a modest, high-confidence margin increment: SEK 65m of savings plus Finland and Denmark holding.
  • They add a moderate, high-confidence capital return: dividend and buybacks.
  • They do not justify a structural step-up in margin.
  • The EPS upside comes from external volume; the internal changes mainly reduce the probability of the old failure modes and introduce a new one (large-project execution).
  • No controlling shareholder will force change, so continuity is the base case.
C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders or other key actors changed, and what does the change signal?

C58

Question asked To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

Judgement. The chief executive has not changed. Almost everyone around him has, in a dated sequence between May 2024 and September 2026. The signal is a deliberate rebuild of the control and capital-deployment layer after the 2024 to 2025 failures. It is not a change of strategy or of operating philosophy. The rebuild is complete but untested at the new scale of contracts.

Who changed.

  • Mattias Johansson: chief executive since 2015, at the company since 1998, born 1973.
  • Petra Vranjes became CFO in May 2025. She came from Telia and Ericsson and replaced Åsa Neving after six years. Neving left after the Region Skåne over-billing case; the sceptical reading is friction over the financial control framework.
  • Lars Täuber became Head of Sweden on 1 January 2026, leading the merged North, Central and South divisions. He had turned around Division South.
  • Christian Alsø has run Denmark since May 2024 (previously chief executive of ISS Denmark). He took the Danish margin from 0.1% to 5.1%.
  • Alexandra Johansson became Head of M&A in 2026, promoted internally.
  • Dennis Bader became Head of Investor Relations in September 2026.
  • Andreas Alsén, a former BCG partner, has been Chief Operations Excellence Officer since 2023 and is a named internal successor candidate.
  • The Head of Division South was dismissed with immediate effect in May 2024, and the Head of North retired.
  • Two directors were added at the 2026 AGM.
  • There is no activist and no blockholder above 10%.

What it means for the model.

  • Execution risk in the regions is lower than in 2023 to 2024: Denmark and Sweden are under proven turnaround leaders.
  • The three most important new roles are all less than 18 months in post: CFO (May 2025), Head of Sweden (January 2026), Head of M&A (2026). They now have to manage something new: principal-contractor data-centre projects and a resumed acquisition programme.
  • Key-person risk stays concentrated in a chief executive with an 11-year tenure, and there is no public external succession plan.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced?

C58

Question asked To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

Judgement. The strategy itself is unchanged: "margin over volume", the Bravida Way (branch managers as owners), branch density, bolt-on acquisitions.

What has changed is posture and project class, in three steps.

  1. From defence to "growth as market conditions permit" (Q4 2025 call, 18 February 2026).
  2. Into large, complex projects (data centres, defence, infrastructure), led by a central special-projects office of about 70 engineers. Bravida acts as a Specialist Package Contractor, and on atNorth as principal contractor including civil works.
  3. Toward capital returns: two SEK 100m buybacks, and the board will keep evaluating more until the 2027 AGM.

How well it is articulated. Well on the opportunity, poorly on risk and economics.

  • No margin, milestone or penalty-clause disclosure has been given on the three data-centre contracts.
  • No timeline has been given for the "above 7%" margin target.
  • The Capital Markets Day of 27 May 2026 reportedly set capital-allocation policies and efficiency targets, but no figures are in the evidence. The next Analyst should obtain them.

How well it is resourced. Adequately for delivery capacity, not for the change in the risk profile.

  • The special-projects office is central.
  • The model delivers through 80% subcontractors.
  • There is still no dedicated internal audit function across about 330 branches.

The strategic judgement that matters for the model. The economic effect of the change is to move revenue mix toward installation and large projects. Morgan Stanley estimates service earns about 100bp more EBITA margin than installation.

  • Sweden's installation share rose from 51% to 53%, and Denmark's from 51% to 55%.
  • So the mix effect works against the margin target, unless the large projects earn above-group margins. That has been asserted (DNB Carnegie: "highly margin accretive", "not margin-dilutive") but not disclosed.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised?

❌ D35

Question asked To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

Judgement. Nothing has been raised.

  • Bravida gives no short-term group guidance.
  • Its medium-term targets are unchanged: sales growth above 5%, EBITA margin above 7%, cash conversion above 100%, net debt to EBITDA below 2.5x, payout of at least 50%.
  • All three operating targets were missed in FY2025: growth -5%, margin 5.9%, cash conversion 79%.
  • The margin target has not been met in at least ten years: the peak was 6.5% in FY2021.
  • Management stopped guiding the Danish margin in basis points once it reached 5%.
  • In early 2025 management said the 7% target was out of reach for FY2025 and pushed "reaching or getting close to" it to FY2026. On clean FY2026 consensus of about 6.3%, that informal timeline has slipped again.

The positive change is in capital-return intent. The second buyback was authorised the day the first ended, and the board's statement that it will "evaluate additional buyback programs continuously until the 2027 AGM" is an unforced step-up.

The incentive hurdles point the same way. The LTIP full-allocation EBITA hurdles are about SEK 1,855m (LTIP 2024), SEK 1,965m (LTIP 2025) and SEK 2,144m (LTIP 2026).

  • If, as seems likely, the LTIP 2026 hurdle refers to the final year of a three-year programme (FY2028), the board's level for full vesting sits about 15% below consensus FY2028 EBIT of SEK 2,507m.
  • The measurement basis is not fully clear in the evidence; verify it.
  • If that reading holds, the board's internal ambition is materially below consensus. That matters for C-68 and C-71.

For the model. There is no guidance anchor to lean on. The model's margin path has to be built bottom-up, not anchored to the "above 7%" target.

C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline?

C60

Question asked To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

Judgement. Standards have improved measurably and fast, but remedially. The fixes respond to the failures of 2023 to 2025: Danish write-downs, Region Skåne over-invoicing, Finnish write-downs, the Northvolt loss. They are not yet an outlier standard, and the control system has not been tested on large turnkey projects.

Execution.

  • Denmark's quarterly EBITA margin went from 0.1% (Q2 2024) to 5.1% (Q2 2026). Full-year EBITA rose from SEK 92m (FY2024) to SEK 370m (FY2025).
  • The Swedish merger came in on time and on its SEK 90 to 100m cost.
  • Group adjusted EBITA rose 20% year on year in Q2 2026.

Risk controls.

  • All tenders above SEK 50m need the chief executive's approval.
  • Branch managers cannot sign above set limits without regional sign-off.
  • A group-wide risk council and a uniform risk taxonomy were introduced.
  • Every branch now gets a randomised audit within a three-year cycle.

Cost discipline. Headcount was cut 4% to about 13,400, branches were closed in southern Sweden, and the Swedish merger targets SEK 65m of annual savings.

Where standards are still weak.

  • There is no dedicated internal audit function.
  • Culture metrics miss their targets:
  • eNPS was 14 in 2025 against a target above 20; Instalco's is 31;
  • unwanted turnover rose to 12.1%;
  • female leaders fell to 10.6% against a 14% target.
  • Group LTIFR was 5.3 in Q2 2026 against a maximum of 5.5, with Denmark at 9.8 and Finland at 7.0.
  • Finland's Q3 2025 margin collapsed to 0.6% on project write-downs in one regional branch. It was 1.3% in Q1 2026 and recovered only to 3.2% in Q2 2026.

For the model. Standards are now good enough to protect the traditional book. They are unproven for the margin risk on SEK 7bn of large projects.

C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units?

C50

Question asked To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

Judgement. Modestly, and not in a way that raises group quality on the numbers.

ABEKA.

  • ABEKA (power distribution and excavation) was sold on 1 April 2026: SEK 472m of 2025 sales, SEK 36m of EBITA.
  • The sale brought SEK 208m of cash, against SEK 300m first estimated, and a SEK 158m gain.
  • It took SEK 564m out of the backlog.
  • ABEKA's margin was 7.6%, above the group's 5.9%. The sale was a focus decision (less technology-intensive work), not an exit from a low-return unit.
  • The arithmetic matters for the model. ABEKA's removal takes about SEK 350m of revenue out of FY2026 (April to December) and about SEK 470m out of FY2027 against FY2025. It also takes about SEK 27m out of FY2026 EBITA and SEK 36m a year thereafter. That is a drag of about 0.1pp on margin, not a lift.

Simplification.

  • Sweden's three divisions became one country organisation.
  • Unprofitable branches in southern Sweden were closed.
  • Finland is restructuring weak regional branches.

No exit is planned from Finland, the one sub-scale market (number four or five, about 4% share), where the margin has been 1% to 3% for a year.

C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly?

C55

Question asked To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

Judgement. There is no new geography: the four Nordic countries are unchanged. The real entry is a new role and project class: principal contractor or turnkey specialist package on large data centres. This is sensible in where it points and risky in how it is structured.

Why it is sensible.

  • The customers are Nordic co-locators: Green Mountain, atNorth, and EcoDataCenter (an eighth project for the same customer).
  • That is the segment where the sector research finds contractors keep pricing power, not standardised hyperscaler packages.
  • Bravida brings about 200MW of references since 2009.
  • Few competitors can staff 40 to 60 white-collar managers per site; only ACG is named.

Why it is risky.

  • Principal-contractor roles (atNorth) take on civil works, design liability, penalty clauses and subcontractor coordination.
  • The earnings review calls this "a major departure from historical low-risk subcontractor service installations".
  • Peers' write-downs in similar large projects are documented: Caverion EUR 59m, Eltel EUR 55m, Sweco SEK 120m. So are Bravida's own: Denmark 2023 to 2024 and Finland 2025.

Smaller product additions: building automation (Elpalko; about SEK 1bn of automation sales), remote monitoring and 5D BIM. These are sensible and incremental.

For the model.

  • The new role justifies a wider margin band on the data-centre revenue: from about 4% (one problem contract) to 8% (clean delivery).
  • It also justifies a working-capital build through 2028 that the traditional business did not need.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management?

❌ D32

Question asked To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

Judgement. Real price rises are not happening in the core. Prices there are at best nominal and lag costs. In the complex-project niche they are latent and increasingly noted. The pricing environment in 2027 will be tested by a wage shock.

Evidence.

  • The installation stage is "heavily commoditised, customers dictate terms and the lowest bidder generally wins" (the Business Foundations report).
  • The chief executive: "Growing in this industry is very easy, they just lower the prices."
  • Index clauses lag spot prices, Swedish contracts lack automatic indexation, and Danish jobs over 12 months are indexed.
  • The ten-year EBITA envelope (5.2% to 6.9%) and a gross margin stuck at 14.2% to 15.2% show no cumulative real pricing.

Where pricing power is latent.

  • In complex work, Bravida "extracts a risk-adjusted premium simply for being a financially stable supplier".
  • For data centres, "execution certainty now matters more than lowest cost" (Bernstein).
  • Management says competition in data centres, defence and infrastructure "is very different".

For the model.

  • Assume price merely tracks cost in the core: 0bp of real price.
  • With Swedish minimum wages up 8% in 2027, even nominal pass-through needs repricing of the traditional book during 2027. If it lags by 9 to 12 months as history suggests, 2027 margins carry a temporary squeeze of perhaps 20 to 40bp before recovering.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they?

❌ D48

Question asked To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

Judgement. M&A is at a trough and stepping up only slowly. The engine is disciplined and well integrated, but it will not be a material EPS driver in 2026 to 2027 at the evidenced pace.

Record.

  • 166 deals since 2014, adding about SEK 12.5bn of sales.
  • Bought at 5 to 6x EBITA before synergies (2 to 3.5x after, by broker estimates).
  • Integrated into the Bravida Way within 3 to 12 months.
  • Target: 5% to 7% acquired growth a year.

Recent pace.

  • 2025: four deals, SEK 454m of acquired sales, about 1.6% of sales.
  • Q1 2026: zero.
  • Q2 2026: SEK 54m spent.
  • Åslunds El (SEK 56m of sales) was added in H2 2026.
  • FactSet acquisition cash was SEK 539m (FY2024), SEK 369m (FY2025) and SEK 113m (H1 2026).

Why it is slow.

  • Management cites target quality ("too few deals") and sellers holding out on price.
  • Private-equity-backed consolidators now bid for the same targets: InstallatørGruppen, Nimlas and ACG.

Strategic sense. High. Bolt-ons build branch density, which is the real moat, at returns of mid-teens post-tax before synergies. Some acquired firms run below group margins, though (Morgan Stanley; the Oras example in Norway).

For the model. Acquired growth of about 1% to 2% a year in 2026 to 2027 rising to 2% to 3% in 2028, not the 5% to 7% target, unless the new Head of M&A demonstrates a step-change. Each 1% of acquired sales at 5.5x EBITA and a 6% margin costs about SEK 95m of cash and adds about SEK 17m of EBITA.

C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet, or stepping up shareholder returns, or is management increasingly mooting either?

C64

Question asked To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

Judgement. Clearly, though in small sizes. The direction is a sustained step-up in returns, with extra distributions likely once the Stavanger cash arrives.

The step-up.

  • First-ever buyback: SEK 100m, 863,100 shares at SEK 115.85 (May to July 2026).
  • Second: SEK 100m, 705,200 shares at SEK 141.79 (August to October 2026; completion per a 20 October filing, unverified).
  • That is about 0.76% of the share count.
  • The dividend was SEK 3.80 for FY2025 (63% payout; SEK 777m) and has grown at 14% a year since the 2015 IPO.
  • The board will "evaluate additional buyback programs continuously until the 2027 AGM".
  • DNB Carnegie expects the Stavanger cash and data-centre prepayments to "trigger additional capital distributions beyond normal dividends".

Capacity.

  • Net debt to EBITDA is 1.3x (company basis), against a ceiling below 2.5x.
  • There are SEK 2.5bn of undrawn revolving facilities to 2028.
  • Capex is below 1% of sales.
  • At 2.5x on FY2026 consensus EBITDA of about SEK 2.7bn, the theoretical headroom is about SEK 2.5 to 3bn (8% to 10% of market capitalisation).
  • Realistically SEK 1 to 2bn is deployable without stretching, given the data-centre working capital and seasonal Q3 cash troughs.

For the model.

  • Assume a dividend of about 55% to 60% of clean EPS; consensus DPS looks low (see the preface).
  • Assume SEK 200 to 400m a year of buybacks, plus a possible one-off distribution of SEK 300 to 500m in 2027 from Stavanger.
  • The EPS effect is small, about 0.5% to 1% a year.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present or plausible?

C60

Question asked What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

Judgement. Four further positive forces are present or plausible. Together they are worth about SEK 0.3 to 0.5 of EPS by FY2027 against FY2025, plus one-off cash.

  1. Finland's recovery from a margin of about 1% to 3% toward 5%. - It is being restructured and has the atNorth contract as a volume base. - On consensus FY2027 revenue of about SEK 3.4bn, every point of margin is about SEK 34m. Moving from 3% to 5% is about SEK 70m.
  2. The full run-rate of the Swedish SEK 65m savings in 2027, with restructuring charges ending in Q3 2026. - Charges were SEK 20m (Q4 2025), SEK 20m (Q1 2026) and SEK 40m (Q2 2026), with about SEK 10 to 20m left.
  3. Legal wins. Stavanger is about SEK 465m of cash, and the counter-suit against Örnsköldsvik municipality is for SEK 50m after police cleared Bravida.
  4. Lower interest cost. Floating SEK debt was repriced down as the Riksbank went from 4.00% to 1.75%, and consensus implicitly assumes much lower net interest (see the preface). This force is now fading as the Riksbank turns.

Plausible but not evidenced.

  • A unified central procurement system, which does not exist yet and which the expert says limits scale benefits.
  • Applying the Danish turnaround playbook to Norway: margin at 5.1% against Sweden's historical 6% to 7%.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present or plausible?

❌ D38

Question asked What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

Judgement. The negative changes are all consequences of the same strategic move: bigger projects inside a decentralised model with thin central control. They cluster on margin risk, cash and governance.

Present.

  1. A sharp rise in the risk profile. - About 30% of the backlog is now with three data-centre customers. - Bravida is principal contractor for the first time at this scale. - Penalty clauses and design liability come with it.
  2. Cash conversion down to 77% (last twelve months, against a 99% ten-year average). The CFO ties it to milestone schedules on large projects, which persist through 2028.
  3. Leadership turnover in finance, IR and M&A while the risk profile rises.
  4. A sale of a higher-margin unit (ABEKA) in order to concentrate on riskier work.
  5. Culture and safety metrics that miss their targets (eNPS 14; unwanted turnover 12.1%; Danish LTIFR 9.8).
  6. No internal audit function, and KPMG has been auditor since 2012.
  7. Incentives driven by EBITA: up to 125% of salary for the chief executive and 200% for other senior managers. These are the incentives the Leadership report links to aggressive local invoicing.
  8. Chief executive pay of SEK 17.27m in 2024, 2.96x Instalco's chief executive, with no discretionary insider buying in twelve months.

Plausible.

  • A write-down on a turnkey data-centre contract.
  • A Stavanger appeal.
  • A repeat municipal over-invoicing case: DNB Carnegie's "tip of the iceberg" watch item, with EY having warned the practice "might be systematic".
  • Loss of skilled staff after the 4% headcount cut, which would cap capacity in the recovery. This was the Risks report's least-monitored failure mode.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors indicated scope for change in strategy or approach?

C50

Question asked To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

Judgement. No controlling actor exists to push for change, so none has signalled any. That is neutral for the case: there is no catalyst and no threat.

  • No shareholder holds more than 10% of capital or votes.
  • Swedish institutional funds hold about 43%.
  • The nomination committee is chaired by Handelsbanken Fonder.
  • There has been no activist campaign in the company's listed history.
  • Directors other than the chief executive hold only 70,745 shares, and two new directors hold none.
  • The board is fully independent but lacks senior installation-industry experience.

The only "actor" pressure is the sell-side's. Deutsche Bank called the first buyback "surprisingly modest" and "formulaic", and the board responded with a second.

For the model. Assume continuity of strategy and capital policy. The probability of a large change in approach (a large acquisition, a leveraged recapitalisation, a break-up) in the next 18 months is low.

C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C56

Judgement. The positive internal changes are real, dated and delivered, but modest in size, and mostly already in the earnings base.

  • What is already in the base: the Danish turnaround was the largest. About three-quarters of the 2025 improvement over 2024 came from Denmark (DNB Carnegie).
  • What is still ahead is small:
  • the SEK 65m Swedish saving (0.2% of revenue);
  • Finland's catch-up (about SEK 50 to 90m);
  • SEK 200m of buybacks a year (0.7% of market capitalisation);
  • extra distributions from SEK 465m of Stavanger cash.
  • Breadth. On the screen's rule, three of twelve forces are strong (second-tier leadership, standards, shareholder returns). The two the screen weights most are absent: a chief executive change and a guidance increase.
  • Resourcing. Adequate for the traditional business. Thin for the new principal-contractor role, which runs through a 70-person central office and 80% subcontracting, with no internal audit.

For the model. Internal change contributes perhaps 0.3 to 0.5pp of margin and 3% to 5% of EPS by FY2027. The external volume change contributes far more. The internal changes make the earnings base cleaner and the company more trustworthy on cost. They do not change its earnings power.

Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4516 questions
C-2462C-2528C-2640C-2752C-2850C-2948C-3142C-8442C-3268C-3362C-3455C-3555C-3658C-3762C-3852C-3933

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judgement. The value chain gives Bravida demand and withholds price.

What it gives.

  • Customers: fragmented and loyal, and now inflecting, with a new concentrated data-centre layer.
  • Regulators and politicians: funding demand.
  • The route to market: direct, which protects the service franchise.
  • The top tier of competitors: rational, for now.

What it withholds.

  • Market structure: the least concentrated in Europe (top five about 18%), with low barriers and a volume-first tail. Prices fall when volume falls in the 50% of revenue that is traditional installation.
  • Suppliers above: concentrated, passing costs straight through.
  • Disruption: concentrated in the segment that carries the growth. Equipment makers and modular systems capture value from the crew.

The precedents agree. In this sector, the money is made on volume returning to a pruned base and on believed scarcity stories. It is lost on project write-downs, leverage and premium multiples meeting flat organic growth. Bravida currently has the first two and the third's setup (premium plus new project risk).

Implications for the second-set model.

  1. Do not model industry-driven margin expansion. Model margin from Bravida's own levers (mix, Finland, Swedish savings, drop-through) against industry cost headwinds (wages, copper).
  2. Model the traditional book at market growth with pro-cyclical, lagged margins.
  3. Model the complex-project segment as share-gaining but carrying wider margin dispersion and higher working capital.
  4. Treat the top tier's discipline as a 2026 to 2027 condition that would weaken if the recovery stalls in 2028.
C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

C62

Judgement. Bravida is the clear number two in the Nordics. Its distance from the number one is moderate, and it varies a lot by country.

Nordic position.

  • Nordic share is about 8.5% (2025), down from 9.0% (2024), on revenue of SEK 28.2bn.
  • The number one is Assemblin Caverion Group (ACG, Triton-owned), with about SEK 41bn of 2025 revenue across nine countries. Its Nordic revenue is probably near Bravida's; it is number one in Finland, Sweden and Norway.
  • Instalco is number three (SEK 13.6bn).
  • Nimlas, at about SEK 10bn, is a fast-growing fourth.

By country.

  • Sweden: number two, about 12% share. It is described elsewhere as co-leader, and is Bravida's strongest market.
  • Denmark: number two, about 10%.
  • Norway: number two, about 6%.
  • Finland: number four or five, about 4%. Instalco grew 30% organically in Finland in 2025 while Bravida's Finnish revenue fell 14%.

Implication for the model.

  • In Sweden and Denmark, Bravida has enough density to be a price-setter on service and mid-sized work.
  • In Finland, the new atNorth contract (about SEK 2.2bn) roughly doubles annual Finnish revenue in 2027 for a business that is sub-scale and running a margin of 1.3% to 3.2%. That is a delivery-risk flag, not just a growth opportunity.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution?

❌ D28

Question asked How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

Judgement. The market is not concentrated and fails both tests.

Size and fragmentation.

  • The market is about SEK 180 to 209bn: Sweden about SEK 130bn, Norway and Finland about SEK 50bn, plus Denmark.
  • It has more than 25,000 vendors.
  • Sub-scale local firms hold 77% to 82%.

The two tests.

  • The top five hold about 18%, against 30% in the test and 39% in France.
  • The top three hold about 23%; the top two just over 10% combined.
  • The distribution is steep only at the very top. Below number five it is a long, flat tail of single-discipline firms with fewer than 20 to 30 staff, so there is no five-times step between positions one to five and six to ten.

Consequence.

  • The top tier cannot set industry price in traditional installation; the marginal price is set by the tail's utilisation.
  • Fragmentation is also the acquisition runway: there are thousands of targets at 5 to 6x EBITA. As the Wisdom Library warns, though, "fragmentation is usually structural, not a roll-up opportunity". Low entry barriers keep re-creating the tail.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

❌ D40

Judgement. Shares are unstable at the top and churning at the bottom. Over three to five years the meaningful gains have gone to private-equity-backed roll-ups, which are "new entrants" by acquisition, not organically.

At the top.

  • Bravida lost 0.5pp of share in 2025 (9.0% to 8.5%). Most of that was deliberate selectivity: about 5% of volume given up to protect margin.
  • But it also lost ground in Finland (-14% against Instalco's +30%).

The roll-ups.

  • ACG was formed in 2024 by Triton combining Assemblin and Caverion.
  • Nimlas grew to 140 companies and about SEK 10bn of revenue, with SEK 1.5bn of acquired sales in 2025.
  • InstallatørGruppen made 48 acquisitions in Denmark and Switzerland since 2023.

At the bottom. Low barriers create continuous churn of small entrants, and their bankruptcies in downturns do not remove capacity.

For the model.

  • Do not assume share gains in the core.
  • Assume Bravida's organic growth tracks the market (about 4% to 5% nominal through the cycle), and that any outperformance comes from complex-project wins and acquisitions.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones, and is this company among them?

C52

Question asked To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

Judgement. Consolidation toward the largest is slow and acquisition-driven. Since about 2010 the top two have reached just over 10% combined. There is no mechanical pull.

Toward the most advantaged. Share is consolidating faster in one segment: complex, capacity-constrained projects (data centres, defence, hospitals, infrastructure).

  • Here the bar is white-collar depth (40 to 60 managers per site), balance-sheet strength and references.
  • The evidence names only Bravida and ACG as qualified Nordic players, and Bravida has just won the region's largest order.

Is Bravida among them? Yes, in that segment. No, in the general market, where it lost share in 2025.

For the model.

  • Complex projects should be modelled as a segment where Bravida gains share, worth perhaps 3 to 5pp of revenue mix over three years.
  • The traditional book should be modelled at market growth.
  • Treat the two consolidation dynamics separately: the first raises growth and risk, the second protects margin.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: margin and return on capital first, or volume first; benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

C50

Question asked What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

Judgement. The attitude splits by tier and segment.

The top tier is margin-first, for reasons of constraint rather than choice.

  • Instalco is at 2.6x leverage and needs margin to cut debt.
  • ACG is private-equity owned, rated B by Fitch and integrating.
  • Bravida runs margin over volume as its stated playbook.

The tail is volume-first. It is willing to bid at zero margin to keep crews utilised, and suffers losses until bankruptcy.

Prices do fall when volume falls in vanilla installation.

  • "The lowest bidder generally wins."
  • 15 bidders per Swedish tender against a historical five.
  • Southern Sweden pricing pressure persisted through 2024 to 2026.
  • A Krüger A/S expert observed reputable installers declining risky tenders in Denmark.

Prices do not fall in complex work. Execution certainty outweighs price there (Bernstein).

For the model.

  • Traditional installation margins should be modelled as pro-cyclical: they rise with utilisation and fall when volume falls, with about a 2 to 4 quarter lag.
  • Service and complex-project margins are steadier.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability, and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

❌ D48

Question asked How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

Judgement. The top tier's margin-first attitude is new (2024 to 2026), was born of distress, and has not been tested in a recovery.

How it changed.

  • Three years ago, Instalco under its previous chief executive chased acquired growth and leverage rose to 3.3x.
  • Caverion and Assemblin competed separately.
  • Bravida itself chased volume in some divisions, as the Leadership report notes.

Why it is not yet stable.

  • The calm depends on balance-sheet constraint.
  • As Instalco de-levers (target below 2.5x) and ACG finishes integrating and grows into data centres (business area set up August 2026), the temptation to chase backlog in a capacity-tight recovery returns.

Nobody shows "O'Leary" instability. No large actor is erratic or reactive.

A "shark attack" by a large player is unlikely. None has the balance sheet or motive.

The realistic attack is diffuse.

  • Desperate price-dumping by small firms if private construction stalls again (DNB Carnegie's warning).
  • New-build contractors moving into renovation.

What would break it. A failed recovery in 2027 to 2028, which the turn in rates makes possible.

For the model. Hold the traditional-book margin flat to slightly up in 2026 to 2027. Run a downside in which a stalled 2028 recovery takes 30 to 50bp off it.

C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

❌ D42

Judgement. The structure is unsupportive of pricing and margin, and supportive of volume and acquisitions.

Why it is unsupportive of pricing.

  • Top five at about 18%.
  • Low entry barriers.
  • A volume-first tail.
  • Concentrated wholesalers above.
  • Fragmented, price-led procurement below: public tenders, and main contractors such as Skanska managing subcontractors "primarily on price".

Why it supports volume and acquisitions.

  • Thousands of targets.
  • A long runway.
  • A rational top tier for now.

What support exists is fragile in two places.

  1. The top tier's discipline, which is constraint-driven and new.
  2. The tail's behaviour, which depends on construction volume.

The one structurally supportive niche is complex projects. There, capability concentrates share, but it is also where the new risk sits.

Implication for the model.

  • The industry's through-cycle EBITA ceiling for good operators is 6% to 8.5%. Instalco historically achieved about 8% on a cost-plus-heavy mix (57% cost-plus or variable contracts).
  • Bravida at 5.9% to 6.2% has room to improve within the structure. But the structure itself will not lift margins; only mix and execution can.
C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

❌ D42

Judgement. In this sector, outperformance has come from three sources, and underperformance from three others.

Sources of outperformance.

  1. Cyclical volume returning to a pruned cost base, with earnings upgrades arriving two to six quarters after construction starts (Sector digest).
  2. A re-rating once the market believes a scarcity or structural story. SPIE's grid exposure; Bravida's own data-centre narrative.
  3. Steady acquisition compounding at 5 to 6x EBITA for listed consolidators trading at 10 to 14x (SPIE's model, Bravida 2015 to 2022, Instalco 2017 to 2021).

Sources of underperformance.

  1. Project write-downs, which arrive suddenly under percentage-of-completion accounting: - Caverion (EUR 59m), Eltel (EUR 55m), Sweco (SEK 120m); - Bravida in Denmark 2023 to 2024 and Finland 2025; - Bilfinger fell 44% over twelve months after its 16 September 2026 warning.
  2. Leverage in a downturn. Instalco at 3.3x; Fasadgruppen's rights issue and its 56% share-price fall.
  3. A premium multiple on flat organic growth. Burkhalter, at 28x earnings with 1% to 3% growth, fell 28% in six months.

Where Bravida sits now.

  • The industry digest's pattern for recovery names: shares re-rated 30% to 70% in three to twelve months, well ahead of single-digit moves in estimates.
  • Bravida is up about 68% in twelve months, while FY2027 consensus EPS is up 10% over twelve months and 17% over six.
  • So Bravida fits "the market has paid for the self-help recovery; the next return depends on organic volume catching up with the price".
  • It fits the sector's best-performing pattern on fundamentals (volume returning to a pruned base, plus a scarcity theme). It also now carries the setup of the worst pattern: a premium multiple plus new large-project risk. In this sector one execution miss after a re-rating has typically cost 20% to 40% (Bilfinger, Fasadgruppen, Burkhalter).

The Wisdom Library's "False Friends" warning. Acquired growth can mask organic decay. It fitted Bravida in Q4 2025: flat sales from +4% acquired against -3% organic. It does not fit now: organic +9% with acquired 0%.

The successor risk to watch. Data-centre growth masking a still-flat traditional book.

C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C68

Judgement. Customer demand supports the volume half of the thesis strongly. Its fragility has changed shape: from cyclical breadth to customer concentration.

The broad base is intact.

  • 84,000 to 91,000 customers.
  • No customer above 5% of revenue; the top five are 11% to 13%.
  • 94% to 98% of revenue is from recurring customers.
  • 91% of customers above SEK 5m renew.
  • End markets: construction 26%, public 21%, other commercial 17%, industrial 16%, property companies 13%, new residential 7%.
  • Service, 48% of sales, held through the downturn.

What is new. About 30% of the backlog now sits with three data-centre customers, all externally financed co-location developers (see C-08).

  • This is the first material customer concentration in Bravida's history.
  • It is exposed to a different risk from the construction cycle: the AI-infrastructure financing cycle and hyperscaler capital spending.

Fragility points.

  1. The developers' access to capital.
  2. Milestone payment terms, which pull working capital in.
  3. Renegotiation or cancellation risk if hyperscaler spending pauses. The earnings review's own reversal risk is "hyperscaler capital expenditure budgets curtailed due to a broader technology sector downturn".

Mitigants. Customer repeat behaviour (EcoDataCenter's eighth project) and a Nordic cost and power advantage that keeps the region attractive.

C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C62

Judgement. The route to market mostly helps.

Why it helps. Bravida sells directly through about 330 branches in 206 locations. It is the "final mile", so disintermediation risk in the core is "virtually zero". It captures the whole downstream service margin pool.

Two intermediaries hinder.

  1. Main contractors. Construction companies are about 26% of sales. Skanska and others increasingly manage subcontractors "primarily on price", which caps margin on new-build installation where Bravida is a subcontractor.
  2. At the data-centre top end, owners procure long-lead equipment directly from manufacturers. That shrinks the installer's scope and margin base (see C-03 and C-38). Bravida's Specialist Package Contractor model is the defence.

A route-to-market tailwind. Grid operators and developers are shifting to three- to five-year framework agreements to secure contractor capacity (expert call, September 2026). That suits Bravida's scale.

Fragility: low in the core, moderate at the top end.

C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

C55

Judgement. On balance, competitors' behaviour helps now and is turning more competitive.

Instalco.

  • It has recovered: organic +8.4%, margin 7.1%, Swedish margin 7.0%, backlog +17% at Q2 2026.
  • It is disciplined but stronger, and it out-grew Bravida in Finland.
  • It is expanding in Germany through Fabri, which diverts its acquisition capital away from the Nordics. That is mildly positive for Bravida's bolt-on pipeline.

ACG.

  • It is the larger number one, has a 7.9% adjusted margin, and is integrating.
  • It has just entered data centres in Sweden as a dedicated business area. That makes it the direct rival for the next round of Nordic data-centre awards.

Private-equity roll-ups (Nimlas, InstallatørGruppen) compete for acquisitions, not for customers. InstallatørGruppen uses a digital procurement platform (Spectia) to win purchasing synergies Bravida does not capture.

Bravida's position against them.

  • Secure in Swedish and Danish service.
  • Strong in complex projects.
  • Weak in Finland.
  • Average on margin: it trails Instalco by about 1pp and ACG by about 2pp.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

C55

Judgement. The threat is low to moderate overall. The three types differ.

  1. Adjacent entrants: a moderate, mid-term threat. - Integrated facility managers (CBRE, JLL) and soft-services firms (Coor, ISS) are pushing into hard technical services. CBRE has stated an intent to buy local installers. - Equipment makers are expanding into services selectively. - These threaten the service book (48% of sales) over three to five years, mostly at large multi-site property portfolios.
  2. Standard entrants: a constant, low-grade threat to price, not share. Low barriers mean continuous churn of small firms, 15 bidders per tender, and zero-margin bids.
  3. Orthogonal or innovative entrants: low now, rising in data centres. - Contractors with in-house prefabrication factories (Peab). - Modular skid suppliers. - International data-centre specialists. Experts say they must acquire locally to gain credibility in the Nordics.

For the model. No entrant changes the 18-month numbers. Adjacent facility managers are the type to monitor for the service margin beyond 2028.

C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives a threat, and how is that changing?

C58

Question asked To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

Judgement. Substitutes for physical installation and maintenance in buildings are limited. What is changing is how the work is done, and that shifts value rather than removing it.

  1. Prefabrication and modular systems. - These are standard in data-centre power and cooling. - They reduce on-site labour content per megawatt. - Bravida has no factory, so it could become "structurally dependent on third-party suppliers".
  2. Remote, predictive and automated maintenance. - This shifts service from visits to monitoring. - Bravida's roughly SEK 1bn automation business and its remote-monitoring capability position it on the right side.
  3. Owners self-performing maintenance. Hyperscalers could eventually internalise data-centre maintenance, but rely on specialists today.

Change. Slow in the core; faster in data centres, where it is a margin threat rather than a volume threat.

C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C62

Judgement. Regulators and politicians help demand and hurt cost and flexibility. The demand help is robust in direction and fragile in timing.

Help.

  • Sweden's defence budget (SEK 175bn) and transport plan (SEK 1,171bn).
  • Finland's NATO-driven building and its EUR 2.8bn transport programme.
  • The EPBD, transposed in Sweden on 1 October 2026 (national renovation plan by end-2026).
  • The EU sub-metering mandate (January 2027).
  • Sweden's fiscal expansion.

Hurt.

  • Post-Skåne municipal audits and contract reviews, which "erode the low-cost decentralised advantage".
  • CSRD compliance.
  • Strict work-environment law, with LTIFR scrutiny: Denmark 9.8 and Finland 7.0.

Fragility.

  • Permitting delays push public projects right.
  • Norway's political gridlock over EU energy directives.
  • Monetary authorities now tightening: the Riksbank and Norges Bank are the most market-moving political actors for 2027 to 2028 volumes.
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting, how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

C52

Question asked On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

Judgement. Disruption is acting on five actors.

  • Demand (the customers). AI and data centres, electrification and building automation raise the technical content and complexity of what is installed. This helps a scale installer.
  • Competition.
  • Prefabrication favours players with factories (Peab).
  • Digital procurement platforms (InstallatørGruppen's Spectia) help roll-ups extract purchasing savings that Bravida does not capture.
  • Instalco's building-automation start-up (Inmatiq) competes for automation work.
  • These are mild negatives.
  • Regulators. Energy-efficiency and sub-metering mandates. A positive.
  • Suppliers.
  • Equipment makers sell directly to data-centre owners, and three-year lead times give them bottleneck power.
  • Skilled labour is scarce and wages rise.
  • This is the most adverse force, because it moves value away from the installer in the very segment carrying the growth.
  • Other actors. Data-centre owners standardise packages, which commoditises installers.

Where this leaves Bravida.

  • Robust in the core: about 90% of revenue.
  • Moderately fragile at the data-centre top end, where it must keep a package-contractor role rather than slide into supplying labour.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

❌ D33

Judgement. Suppliers are a source of risk, and the risk is rising.

Materials suppliers are concentrated and pass inflation straight through.

  • Ahlsell holds 21% of Nordic and 30% of Swedish installation distribution.
  • Sonepar, Rexel and Onninen control 82% of Swedish electrical distribution.
  • Lindab's margins fell, and peers such as Systemair are raising prices.
  • Insulation makers have raised prices 31% to 37%.
  • Copper is forecast up 38% in 2026.
  • Equipment lead times are about three years.

Bravida has little leverage over them.

  • A central procurement team of 50 to 60.
  • No unified procurement IT system.
  • Strong branch autonomy in purchasing.

The subcontractor tier carries 80% of data-centre labour, and it is fragile.

  • Thousands of small firms, often with foreign staff.
  • Bankruptcies are high (Finland had 841 construction bankruptcy applications in 2024).
  • Only one supplier was audited in 2025.

Mitigants.

  • Approved-supplier frame agreements.
  • Back-to-back warranties.
  • Index clauses.
  • Early material purchasing.

For the model. Supplier behaviour is the main reason to shade the margin on the data-centre contracts and the 2027 traditional book below consensus, until the company shows price pass-through.
