Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Bravida Holding AB (BRAV-SE, Nasdaq Stockholm): a Nordic multi-technical installation and service contractor (electrical, heating, plumbing, ventilation and air conditioning, security, building automation), reporting four country segments (Sweden, Denmark, Norway, Finland) with sales split between project installation (about 52%) and recurring service and maintenance (about 48%) in FY2025, and now delivering large data-centre contracts for Green Mountain (Norway), atNorth (Finland) and EcoDataCenter (Sweden).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Instalco (INSTAL-SE; closest listed Nordic decentralised installer, running a Swedish margin turnaround under "Instalco 2.0"); Assemblin Caverion Group (private, Triton-owned; the Nordic number one, post-merger synergy programme and a new Swedish data-centre business area from August 2026); SPIE (SPIE-FR; European multi-technical leader whose margin rose from 6.7% in 2023 to 7.6% in 2025 through mix and selectivity); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; industrial services peer whose September 2026 profit warning is a recent operating-deleverage precedent); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; decentralised Swiss installer integrating HVAC acquisitions); Fasadgruppen (FG-SE; decentralised Nordic contractor showing negative operating leverage in the 2026 winter downturn); Comfort Systems USA (FIX; US mechanical contractor whose data-centre exposure coincided with margin expansion); EMCOR Group (EME; US electrical and mechanical contractor with large data-centre installation work) Inputs: What share of Bravida's production cost is own labour, subcontracted labour and materials, by country, and what share of its selling and administrative costs (about 9.3% of sales in FY2025) is fixed. Why did selling and administrative costs grow faster than revenue between FY2021 and FY2025. How did Bravida's EBITA margin move in the 2022 volume surge (revenue +20%) and in the 2024–2025 volume decline (organic -5% in 2025), and what explains each move. What incremental margin did the Q1 2026 and Q2 2026 reports show against the prior-year quarters, and what drove it. On large turnkey and package contracts delivered with about 20% own staff and 80% subcontractors (DNB Carnegie on Green Mountain, June 2026), what EBITA margins have Bravida and peers actually earned, and has subcontractor-heavy delivery diluted or protected margins. Has the Swedish country merger (SEK 90–100 million of restructuring costs; SEK 65 million of targeted annual savings; company, February 2026) delivered on time, and how fast are the savings ramping. What did the Danish turnaround (0.1% EBITA margin in Q2 2024 to 5.1% in Q2 2026) actually change in costs and pricing, and is the same playbook being applied in Finland (EBITA margin 1.3% in Q1 2026, 3.2% in Q2 2026). What have peers' cost programmes delivered against what was promised: Instalco's 2025 FTE cuts and branch closures, Assemblin Caverion's merger synergies, Fasadgruppen's removal of a management layer, SPIE's integration of acquisitions. Is a unified central procurement system feasible at Bravida (central procurement team of 50–60 people per an expert call), and what did procurement programmes deliver at peers. How will the 2027 Swedish collective wage round affect installers' fixed-price contracts signed in 2026. Which project write-down precedents (Bravida Denmark 2023–2024, Bravida Finland Q3 2025, Caverion EUR 59 million, Eltel EUR 55 million, Sweco SEK 120 million) show where fixed-price project margins break. Focus: Whether Bravida's largely variable cost base can turn the revenue growth now contracted, from data-centre awards above SEK 7 billion and the Nordic construction recovery, into an EBITA margin above its ten-year range of 5.2% to 6.9%. And which cost lines beyond the SEK 65 million Swedish programme can realistically be reduced without damaging the decentralised branch model.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Instalco (INSTAL-SE; same Nordic tender markets, 57% cost-plus or variable contracts and an ABC customer programme); Assemblin Caverion Group (private; largest Nordic competitor and now a direct rival for data-centre work); SPIE (SPIE-FR; indexation clauses and daily pricing tools across multi-year framework contracts); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; dynamic pass-through of metal costs in a labour-scarce market); Fasadgruppen (FG-SE; fixed-price renovation contractor exposed to material inflation); Comfort Systems USA (FIX; pricing on data-centre mechanical work); Nimlas (private Nordic consolidator competing for the same local work and targets) Operating conditions: a Nordic construction recovery coinciding with a new Swedish collective wage round in 2027 and scarce skilled electricians; a renewed rate-hiking cycle (Riksbank signalling hikes in autumn 2026; Norges Bank at 4.50% after September 2026) slowing private construction; rising copper prices and roughly three-year lead times for switchgear and transformers on fixed-price contracts; large turnkey or package contracts for externally financed Nordic co-location data-centre developers; public-sector framework tenders after the Region Skåne over-invoicing case; renewal of multi-year service and maintenance agreements Inputs: What share of Bravida's revenue is fixed-price, cost-plus and index-linked, by country, given that Swedish contracts lack automatic indexation while Danish jobs over 12 months are indexed (Deutsche Bank and DNB Carnegie). How long does cost pass-through take in practice (a 9–12 month lag is cited). What happened to Bravida's and peers' margins during the 2022–2023 inflation episode, and which contracts were affected. Is there evidence of price increases achieved and held by Nordic installers since 2022, and of price given back in 2024–2025. What escalation, prepayment, milestone and penalty terms are typical in Nordic data-centre installation contracts, and what is publicly known about the terms on Green Mountain (about NOK 4.5 billion, joint venture with Backe Industri), atNorth (about EUR 200 million, principal contractor including civil works) and EcoDataCenter (SEK 650 million). How many bidders now compete on Swedish installation tenders (about 15 against a historical 5, per Instalco research, October 2026). Are smaller firms still bidding at zero margin, and are new-build contractors moving into renovation. How sticky are service contracts (three- to seven-year terms, warranty lock-in), and what churn do customers and experts report. What do customers, competitors and the sell-side say now about installers' ability to pass on the 2027 wage round and copper costs. What equipment-maker and wholesaler price increases have been announced for 2026–2027 (Systemair, Lindab, Ahlsell, Rexel, Onninen). Focus: Whether Bravida can raise prices at least in line with labour and material costs in 2027–2028, especially in Sweden where contracts are not automatically indexed. And whether its large data-centre contracts carry escalation, prepayment and risk-allocation terms that protect margin if costs or schedules move against it.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Several dynamics in Bravida's model can feed on themselves or reverse together, and their direction decides whether earnings keep growing after the current data-centre contracts complete in 2028–2029 or step down. They are: repeat data-centre awards built on references; customer advances funding near-term cash; and labour scarcity against wage resets and tail competitors' pricing. Peers: Comfort Systems USA (FIX; the clearest precedent of a data-centre reference loop compounding backlog); EMCOR Group (EME; large-project data-centre installer); SPIE (SPIE-FR; deliberately avoids standardised hyperscaler contracts in favour of complex co-location and grid work); Assemblin Caverion Group (private; now building a dedicated data-centre unit in Sweden); Instalco (INSTAL-SE; the same Nordic labour and tender markets); Bilfinger (GBF-DE; a precedent of a services group whose momentum reversed in 2026) Inputs: A reinforcing loop: does delivering data-centre projects (about 200 MW since 2009; an eighth project for EcoDataCenter in July 2026) win further awards and deepen white-collar project capability, and how did that loop run at Comfort Systems USA and EMCOR. A competitive limit to that loop: how is it affected by Assemblin Caverion's new data-centre business area and by operators buying power and cooling equipment directly from manufacturers. A balancing loop in cash: do customer advances on large contracts lift cash conversion early and depress it later. Bravida's company-basis cash conversion moved from 112% in mid-2024 to 77% in 2026, attributed by the CFO to advances being used during execution. A reinforcing loop in operating leverage: does volume returning to a cost base cut by about 4% in headcount raise margins, and for how long. A balancing loop in pricing: does a recovering market fill smaller competitors' idle capacity and stop price-dumping, and would a stalled recovery restart it. A balancing loop in labour: does skilled-labour scarcity lift wages faster than fixed-price contracts can reprice. A loop in customer financing: how does the financing of Nordic co-location developers (Green Mountain, atNorth, EcoDataCenter) respond to credit conditions in AI-infrastructure funding, and how would that feed into Bravida's backlog and receivables (precedent: Northvolt receivables written down in 2024). A reflexive loop: how do share-price moves affect the cost of Bravida's buybacks and the competition for bolt-on acquisitions from private-equity consolidators such as InstallatørGruppen and Nimlas. Focus: Whether the data-centre reference loop is strong enough to refill Bravida's large-project backlog before the Green Mountain, atNorth and EcoDataCenter contracts complete in 2028–2029. And whether working-capital, wage and competitor-pricing dynamics will reverse the 2026–2027 gains in margin and cash.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Bravida's earnings per share depend on several inputs that brokers treat differently: one-off gains and restructuring, net interest including leases, the dividend payout, currency translation and the phasing and margin of three large contracts. A line-by-line bridge from EBITA to earnings per share is therefore needed. Peers: Instalco (INSTAL-SE; Nordic installer whose net financial items absorb a large share of EBIT); SPIE (SPIE-FR; multi-technical peer with disclosed drop-through, tax-rate and share-count effects); Burkhalter (BRKN-CH; high-payout installer with a near-zero hedging policy); Comfort Systems USA (FIX; reference for data-centre contract phasing into reported revenue and margin) Inputs: How each broker treats the SEK 158 million ABEKA capital gain (Q2 2026) and the SEK 90–100 million Swedish restructuring costs in FY2026 earnings per share (FactSet median FY2026 EPS SEK 7.20 at 13 October 2026). The split of FY2025 net interest expense (SEK 181 million per FactSet) between lease interest and bank debt, the fixed-or-floating mix (company: 100% floating, all in SEK), and the effect of the Riksbank and Norges Bank rate paths on 2027–2028 interest cost. The effective tax rate outlook (20.8% in FY2025) and whether the ABEKA gain is tax-exempt. The share count path after buybacks of 863,100 shares (completed July 2026) and 705,200 shares (to October 2026), net of conversions of Class C incentive shares. The revenue phasing and expected margins of the Green Mountain, atNorth and EcoDataCenter contracts, including whether the NOK 4.5 billion Green Mountain figure is Bravida's share or the joint venture's total. The Finnish margin path and the ramp of the SEK 65 million Swedish savings. The sensitivity of EPS to translation (a 10% stronger krona against NOK and DKK cited at about SEK 0.28 of EPS in the company-based Risks report, June 2026). The dividend payout policy (at least 50%; 63% for FY2025) against FactSet consensus dividends of SEK 4.00, 4.25 and 4.40 for FY2026–FY2028. The measurement period and level of the LTIP 2026 EBITA hurdle (at least SEK 2,144 million) and what it implies about internal planning. Any figures set at the 27 May 2026 Capital Markets Day for capital allocation and efficiency. The timing and appeal status of the Stavanger hospital payment (about NOK 330 million plus NOK 150 million of interest and costs, June 2026 ruling). The expected outcome and timing of the Danish public-sector arbitration (about SEK 800 million, 2028). The expected contribution of bolt-on acquisitions under the new Head of M&A. Focus: Pin down the FY2026–FY2028 bridge from EBITA to earnings per share, covering one-off items, net interest, tax, share count, currency and payout. And establish the margin and phasing of the three data-centre contracts, so that the earnings path can be built line by line rather than taken from a consensus whose brokers define these items differently.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 11,883 words · written 21 October 2026, 18:12 to 18:32 UK, Opus, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Bravida (BRAV-SE): second set of questions, the operating and financial model

Written 2026-10-21 for the Investment Analyst answering the third and fourth sets. Money in SEK millions unless stated; per-share figures in SEK. "Adjusted EBITA" means the company's EBITA before items affecting comparability (the ABEKA disposal gain, the Swedish restructuring charges and, if it is booked in 2026, the interest and cost award on the Stavanger hospital case).

Before the answers: what has changed since the first set, and the base case in one table

Six corrections to the first set's preface and answers

These change the bridge from operating profit to earnings per share, and two of them change how the evidence on internal planning and customer risk should be read.

  1. FactSet's free cash flow line deducts dividends, not lease repayments. The first set hypothesised lease repayments. The arithmetic holds exactly in all seven years: operating cash flow less capital spending less dividends paid equals FactSet's free cash flow (FY2025: 1,453 − 156 − 767 = 530; FY2024: 1,896 − 54 − 714 = 1,128; FY2023: 1,417 − 113 − 662 = 642; FY2019: 1,599 − 34 − 404 = 1,161). - So the "43% free cash flow over net income" for FY2025 is after the dividend and is not a cash-quality measure. - Before dividends and before lease repayments it was 105% (FY2025), 174% (FY2024) and 106% (FY2023). - After an estimated SEK 430 to 450m of lease principal repayments, which is the measure I use, it was about 70% (FY2025), about 135% (FY2024) and about 70% (FY2023).
  2. The FY2025 net financial cost was about SEK 105 to 110m, not SEK 181m. - FactSet's SEK 181m is gross interest expense. - The profit and loss arithmetic gives the net figure: reported EBITA of about SEK 1,660m (adjusted SEK 1,680 to 1,690m less SEK 20m of restructuring) less pre-tax profit of SEK 1,559m leaves about SEK 100 to 110m. DNB Carnegie (28 September 2026) gives SEK −109m, of which lease interest is SEK −50m. - So the consensus pre-tax line, which implies only SEK 60 to 67m a year, is about SEK 40 to 55m too light. That is worth about SEK 0.15 to 0.20 of EPS, not the SEK 0.5 the first set's comparison implied. - Amortisation of acquired intangibles is close to nil on the same arithmetic, so EBIT and EBITA are effectively the same line for this company. - The company's own sensitivity (2025 annual report) is SEK 15m of profit for each percentage point on the loan rate, below the first set's SEK 25 to 30m. Rate risk to EPS is trivial: about SEK 0.06 a share per percentage point.
  3. The LTIP 2026 hurdle does not show internal planning below consensus. - The first set read the SEK 2,144m full-allocation EBITA hurdle as a FY2028 level, 15% below consensus. The size of the hurdles only makes sense as a single-year EBITA level: SEK 1,855m (LTIP 2024) and SEK 1,965m (LTIP 2025) sat 16% to 17% above the FY2024 and FY2025 outcomes (about SEK 1,585m and SEK 1,690m), with nothing allocated below 85% of target. - The AlphaSense earnings-bridge report also reads LTIP 2026 as measured on FY2026 EBITA. - On that reading the full-allocation level sits about 7% above FY2026 consensus EBIT (SEK 2,002m) and about 10% above my adjusted FY2026 EBITA of SEK 1,942m. The 85% threshold, SEK 1,822m, is comfortably met. If the board counts reported EBITA, the tax-free ABEKA gain lifts FY2026 to about SEK 2,025m, 94% of target. - Conclusion: the hurdle is a stretch level set above outcomes. It says nothing reliable about internal planning, and the first set's inference that the board's ambition sits materially below consensus should be dropped. The Leadership digest's wording ("cumulative group EBITA targets over three years") leaves residual ambiguity; it is a verification item, not a signal.
  4. Customer-financing risk is lower for two of the three data-centre customers than the first set framed it. - atNorth was bought by Equinix and CPP Investments for USD 4bn, completed 2 September 2026. It is no longer a Partners Group-owned developer. - EcoDataCenter raised EUR 600m of private credit from Deutsche Bank (September 2025) and a SEK 1,000m green bond (ABG Sundal Collier, 2 October 2026). - Green Mountain (Azrieli-owned) is the least evidenced, with EUR 371m of financing reported. - The financing-crack risk the first set raised is real for the next round of awards. It is weaker for the contracts already in the order book.
  5. The largest contract is cost-plus and the stated value is Bravida's own share. - Green Mountain (NOK 4.5bn, about SEK 4.3bn) is Bravida's share of the DC Nordic joint venture with Backe Industri (company press release, 8 June 2026). - DNB Carnegie (18 June 2026) describes the contract as cost-plus, with prepayments expected on Green Mountain and atNorth. - The margin risk on data centres therefore concentrates in atNorth: a principal-contractor role including civil works, about SEK 2.2bn, in Finland, the group's weakest unit. It does not concentrate in the largest contract.
  6. The wage shock is smaller than the first set assumed. - SEB's "+8% for 2027" is the rise in agreed minimum wages. - Nordea (2 September 2026) expects Swedish wage agreements just below 3.0% a year from the spring 2027 round, and Swedbank (24 August 2026) expects about 3.2% a year for 2027 to 2028. - Bravida prices expected wage increases into tenders (stated practice since at least 2018), and actual pay for skilled installers sits well above agreed minimums. - I therefore model a 2027 squeeze of about SEK 25m (about 7bp), not 20 to 40bp. The tail case, settlements above about 4%, is about SEK 60 to 80m.

Two new facts the first set did not have; both need verification

  1. A possible fourth data-centre award. The AlphaSense feedback-loop report cites a company press release of 25 August 2026, "Bravida awarded installation contract for a new data center in Sweden worth approximately 850 MSEK". It is not in any first-set source. - If it is real, data-centre awards since June 2026 total about SEK 8bn, and the reference loop is still producing awards. - I give it half weight in FY2027 to FY2028 revenue.
  2. A possible quantified framework from the Capital Markets Day. Morgan Stanley (16 October 2026 preview) attributes to a "June Capital Markets Day" a roadmap of: - 10% a year EPS growth; - 4% to 6% organic growth; - 20 to 30bp of margin improvement a year; - about 1% a year of EPS from capital allocation.

Bravida's Capital Markets Day was 27 May 2026, so the attribution is uncertain.

  • If the roadmap is Bravida's, it is the first quantified anchor the company has given. From FY2025's 6.0% it implies 6.6% to 6.9% by FY2028: in line with my base case and below consensus's 7.1%.

Points where I agree with the first set but the size differs

  • Clean FY2026 EPS is about SEK 7.1, not SEK 6.5 to 6.9.
  • DNB Carnegie's FactSet entry (SEK 6.96) is already clean. DNB shows a separate net one-off gain of SEK 98m that takes its reported figure to about SEK 7.40.
  • The clean broker estimates cluster at SEK 6.96 to 7.13; Deutsche Bank's SEK 6.50 includes the restructuring.
  • So the FY2026 consensus of SEK 7.20 is only slightly flattered, about SEK 0.1. FY2026 growth of about +21% on the brokers' FY2025 base is largely genuine.
  • The gap to consensus lies in FY2027 to FY2028, not FY2026.
  • The first set's FY2027 EBITA (about SEK 2.26bn) was close to mine (SEK 2.19bn). The difference is that I let the extra profit on each extra krona of revenue fade as idle capacity is used up (see P-01).

The base case in one table (detail in P-10 and the forecast inputs)

Financial forecasts

❌ D489 questions
P-0147P-0262P-0350P-0442P-0547P-0640P-0858P-0952P-1050

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Judgement. The financial forecasts describe a better company over the next four years than over the last six, but not the one consensus models.

  • Revenue. The cyclical recovery is real and broad (Q2 2026 organic growth +9%, every country positive). The data-centre contracts add about SEK 3bn of revenue at the 2027 peak. Revenue to FY2027 is therefore well founded and in line with consensus.
  • Margin. Bravida's cost base is about 90% variable, its prices only pass through costs, and its new growth leg is subcontracted and in its largest part cost-plus. So the margin follows the volume recovery only while idle capacity lasts, and then settles about 10bp a year higher.
  • The result:
  • adjusted EPS of SEK 7.11 (FY2026), 8.11 (FY2027) and 8.51 (FY2028), +21%, +14% and +5%;
  • about 8% to 10% below consensus in FY2027 to FY2028;
  • a plateau into FY2029 as the data-centre contracts complete faster than a capacity-limited reference loop can refill them.

What makes the forecasts more trustworthy than the average installer's.

  • Low operating leverage protects the downside on volume.
  • Below-EBIT items are small and predictable, and rates barely matter.
  • The balance sheet is under-used.
  • About half of revenue is service with indexation.
  • Management's record on bounded fixes (Denmark, the Swedish merger) is good.

What makes them less trustworthy.

  • The margin target has never been met, and margin misses historically arrive 12 to 18 months into an upswing.
  • The first principal-contractor role at scale sits in Finland, the weakest unit.
  • Cash will swing sharply between FY2026 and FY2027.
  • Consensus itself is on mixed bases until early 2027.

The steer to the third and fourth sets.

  • Treat revenue to FY2027 as predictable and margin as the variable.
  • Treat FY2027 free cash flow as the most likely disappointment against consensus.
  • Treat the FY2028 to FY2029 plateau as a structural consequence of the contract book and the capacity ceiling, not a tail risk.
  • The near-term prints (Q3 and Q4 2026) are set up to look strong on volume and cash. They are not a test of the 7% margin thesis; that test comes in the 2027 prints.
P-01

After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

❌ D47

Judgement. Bravida's fixed-cost operating leverage is genuinely low, about 8% to 11% of the cost base. The high incremental margin now visible is a feature of the recovery, not of the business model.

  • Why the incremental margin is high now. Volume is returning to a cost base cut by 4% in headcount, with idle crews still available in southern Sweden.
  • When it ends. Once that slack is used, which on the volume path happens during 2027, each extra krona of revenue must again be staffed at rising wages. The share of each extra krona of revenue that reaches EBITA (the "drop-through") then falls back toward the group's average margin.
  • What this means for margin over the next four years. Operating leverage adds about 40bp to the margin in FY2026, about 20bp in FY2027 and little after. It is not the mechanism that takes the margin from 6% to 7%.
  • The data-centre growth leg has even less operating leverage. It runs with 20% own staff and 80% subcontractors, and the largest contract is cost-plus. Its revenue arrives at a fixed contract margin, so it adds EBITA in proportion to revenue but dilutes the group percentage slightly.

The cost structure, from the evidence.

  • Morgan Stanley (26 November 2015): 42% of costs fully flexible (including 12% subcontractors) and 47% semi-flexible (three to six months' notice for operatives), leaving about 11% fixed.
  • Management (Q1 2020 call): only 8% to 9% of the cost base is fixed.
  • Selling and administrative costs are about 9.3% of sales, and part of them flexes too.
  • The history agrees.
  • FY2022: revenue +20% (organic +16% in Q4), margin flat at 6.4% to 6.5%.
  • FY2023: revenue +12%, margin down to 5.9% (Danish write-downs).
  • FY2025: revenue −4.9%, margin up 0.6pp to 6.0% through selective bidding and branch closures.
  • In the last up-cycle the incremental margin was close to the average margin, not double it.

What the current incremental margin really is.

  • Q2 2026: adjusted EBITA +SEK 74m on revenue +SEK 655m, about 11%.
  • Strip out the Swedish savings (about SEK 16m a quarter) and the loss of ABEKA (about SEK 118m of revenue and SEK 9m of EBITA a quarter): the core business turned about SEK 770m of extra revenue into about SEK 67m, about 9%.
  • H1 2026 overall: about 14%, flattered by a weak Q1 2025 base.
  • This is the "volume returning to a pruned cost base" pattern the Sector digest describes. It is real, and it is finite.

Why it fades, and when.

  1. The 4% headcount cut is a one-time capacity buffer. The chief executive said on the Q2 2026 call that slack remains "especially in southern Sweden". Core organic growth of about 5% in FY2026 and about 4.5% in FY2027 uses it up during 2027.
  2. About half of nominal organic growth from 2027 is price, which passes through cost inflation (wages about 3% to 4.5% a year: Norway's 2026 agreement was 4.5%). Price that only passes through cost carries roughly the average margin, not volume-leverage margin.
  3. Labour is the binding constraint in the Nordics: Danish installer unemployment is 1% to 2% (DNB Carnegie, 24 July 2026), and data-centre sites pay 10% to 15% wage premiums for experienced team leaders (former Bravida Sverige chief executive, 13 July 2026). New volume beyond the slack has to be bought at a higher marginal cost.

What I model.

  • Core business (excluding data-centre contracts and ABEKA):
  • incremental margin on the volume part of growth about 13% in H2 2026, 12% in 2027 and 7% to 12% after;
  • incremental margin on the price part at the average margin (about 6.6% to 6.7%).
  • The resulting core EBITA margin: about 6.0% (FY2025), 6.43% (FY2026), 6.59% (FY2027), 6.68% (FY2028), then flat at about 6.7%.
  • Data-centre contracts at about 5.25% net of a small risk allowance in FY2027 to FY2028 (6% before it), about 6% thereafter.
  • Group adjusted EBITA margin: 6.39% (FY2026), 6.46% (FY2027), 6.55% (FY2028), 6.64% (FY2029), 6.67% (FY2030).

The asymmetry this creates.

  • On the way up, operating leverage is modest because costs are variable.
  • On the way down the same variability protects the margin. FY2025 proved this: margin rose on a −5% organic year.
  • The downside that matters is therefore not volume deleverage of the Bilfinger kind. Bilfinger lost 100bp of margin on less than 3% lower revenue because it held its own skilled labour (Deutsche Bank, 17 to 18 September 2026). The downside that matters is project gross-margin loss: write-downs and fixed-price cost overruns.

Against consensus.

  • Consensus's FY2027 EBIT of SEK 2,370m (7.0%) needs about 12% drop-through on the whole SEK 5.7bn revenue increase against FY2025, including data-centre revenue at or above the group margin, and it needs that in a year when the slack runs out.
  • My path delivers about 9% drop-through on the same increase (SEK 2,190m − 1,690m on SEK 5.7bn).
  • About SEK 170m (7%) of EBITA separates us in FY2027, and SEK 230m (9%) in FY2028.
  • Almost all of the gap is the assumed persistence of recovery-phase drop-through, plus the data-centre margin. It is not revenue: my revenue is within 1% of consensus in FY2026 to FY2027.

The range.

  • For FY2027 EBITA, SEK 2.05bn (drop-through 6%, data-centre margin 4%) to SEK 2.40bn (drop-through 14%, data-centre margin 7.5%). That corresponds to margins of about 6.0% to 7.1%.
  • Consensus sits at the top of that range.
P-02

After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

C62

Judgement. Financial leverage barely touches EPS, and that will not change in the forecast period.

  • Interest is a small, stable cost of about SEK 105 to 125m a year, about 5% of EBITA, and rate sensitivity is about SEK 0.06 a share per percentage point.
  • The balance sheet's real role is as an under-used funding source. On my cash path, bank net debt excluding leases stays below 0.8x EBITDA through FY2030 even after SEK 300 to 400m a year of buybacks and SEK 400 to 550m a year of acquisitions.
  • The financial-leverage question is therefore really a capital-allocation question. Each additional SEK 1bn deployed in buybacks at about SEK 155 would add about 3% to EPS. The board has so far chosen SEK 100m programmes.

The structure.

  • The company's net debt to EBITDA was 1.1x including leases and 0.6x excluding them at end-2025, and 1.3x including leases at Q2 2026, a seasonal peak after the SEK 777m dividend. The policy ceiling is below 2.5x.
  • Lease liabilities are about SEK 1.1 to 1.2bn: vehicles (now about 50% electric) and premises. Lease interest is about SEK 50m a year (DNB Carnegie).
  • Bank debt is 100% floating and in SEK. SEK 2.5bn of revolving facilities are undrawn to 2028.
  • Peers: Instalco carries 2.6x, with net financial items absorbing a large share of EBIT; Fasadgruppen 3.3x to 4.2x. Bravida's balance sheet is the strongest in its Nordic cohort.

The forward path, from my base-case cash flows.

Year-end FY2025 FY2026 FY2027 FY2028 FY2029 FY2030
Bank net debt excluding leases about 1,380 about 870 about 1,690 about 1,870 about 2,000 about 2,380
Bank net debt over adjusted EBITDA 0.6x 0.3x 0.6x 0.6x 0.7x 0.8x
Including leases (about SEK 1.2 to 1.3bn) 1.1x about 0.8x about 1.0x about 1.1x about 1.1x about 1.2x
  • FY2026 deleverages on the Stavanger receipt (about SEK 465m) and data-centre prepayments.
  • FY2027 re-levers as the prepayments are consumed and revenue grows by SEK 3.5bn.
  • Even the FY2027 working-capital outflow (about SEK 860m) leaves the company at 1.0x including leases, a quarter of its stated capacity.

Net financial items. I model SEK −105m (FY2026), −120m (FY2027) and −125m a year from FY2028.

  • Why the cost rises.
  • Lease interest grows with the fleet and premises (SEK 50m to about 60m).
  • The Riksbank is expected to raise its policy rate from 1.75%: Nordea expects +25bp in November 2026, and Deutsche Bank expects 2.50% by mid-2027.
  • Average bank debt rises after the FY2027 working-capital outflow.
  • Why it rises only a little. The floating debt is small, and the company's sensitivity is SEK 15m per percentage point.
  • Excluded: the interest and cost element of the Stavanger award (about SEK 145m within the SEK 465m) is treated as an item affecting comparability if it is booked in H2 2026.
  • Against consensus. The consensus profit lines imply about SEK 62 to 67m a year. DNB Carnegie's own model is near SEK 110m. The median lines are internally inconsistent, as the first set found. The difference to mine is about SEK 0.15 to 0.25 of EPS a year, small beside the margin gap.

Currency. All debt is in SEK while 53% of sales is in NOK, DKK and EUR, unhedged.

  • A 10% stronger krona against NOK and DKK takes about SEK 0.28 off EPS (company, June 2026), about 3% of FY2027 EPS. It also lowers leverage mechanically, because foreign EBITDA shrinks but no foreign debt does.
  • I assume about −0.5% of translation drag on revenue in H2 2026 and FY2027, and none after. Consensus brokers assume EUR/SEK of 10.20 to 10.50.

Where the balance sheet could matter more than I model.

  1. Upside: a larger distribution. With about SEK 1.5bn of bank-debt headroom below 1.0x excluding leases, a SEK 1bn special buyback or extraordinary dividend in 2027 is feasible. It would add about 2.5% to 3% to EPS. - DNB Carnegie expects the Stavanger cash and prepayments to "trigger additional capital distributions beyond normal dividends". Deutsche Bank called the first programme "surprisingly modest". - I model SEK 400m in FY2027 (including the Stavanger proceeds), not SEK 1bn, because the board's record is conventional and incremental: two SEK 100m programmes, executed at rising prices.
  2. Downside: the 2028 Danish arbitration (SEK 800m receivable). - A loss would be a one-off write-down of up to about SEK 3.1 a share after tax and a non-cash hit to equity. Leverage would hardly move, because the cash was never received. - A win would be about SEK 800m of cash, a likely further distribution. - Management says it expects no significant earnings impact. I keep it out of the base case as a binary for FY2028.

Effect on EPS. Net financial items take about 5% to 6% of EBITA in every forecast year, against about 6% in FY2025 and 12% to 13% in FY2023 to FY2024 when Swedish rates peaked. Financial leverage is neither a tailwind nor a headwind to EPS growth. The EPS contribution from the balance sheet comes through the share count (buybacks of about 1.0% to 1.3% of shares a year, after the small dilution from incentive share conversions), worth about 1% a year of EPS growth.

P-03

After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

C50

Judgement. Identifiable cost elimination is about SEK 65m of contracted Swedish savings, plus perhaps SEK 30 to 60m of further, unannounced efficiencies by FY2028. In total that is 0.3 to 0.4pp of margin, about half of it already in the FY2026 run-rate. The rest arrives in FY2027.

  • Beyond that, the decentralised branch model limits large cuts: the model is the source of its low fixed costs, so cutting it removes the advantage.
  • The bigger margin lever is not cost elimination. It is regional catch-up: Finland from about 3% toward 4.5%, Norway and Denmark edging up. That catch-up is turnaround execution, which the company has a record of delivering one region at a time.

What is contracted and when it lands.

  • The Swedish merger. Three divisions became one country organisation on 1 January 2026.
  • Restructuring cost: SEK 90 to 100m in total. SEK 20m (Q4 2025), SEK 20m (Q1 2026) and SEK 40m (Q2 2026) are booked, with about SEK 10 to 20m left for Q3 2026, the last charge.
  • Savings: SEK 65m a year "starting in 2026", ramp not disclosed.
  • My phasing: about SEK 40m inside FY2026 and the remaining SEK 25m in FY2027. That is about 13bp of group margin in FY2026 and about 7bp more in FY2027.
  • The reported-to-adjusted gap closes after Q3 2026. From Q4 2026 reported and adjusted EBITA should coincide, except for the possible Stavanger interest element.

What is plausible but not announced (SEK 30 to 60m by FY2028; I include about SEK 15m a year from FY2028 within the regional catch-up).

  • Digital-modernisation spend of SEK 80 to 100m a year described as "past its peak": perhaps SEK 20 to 40m lower by FY2028.
  • Branch consolidation in Finland and Norway along the Swedish lines: SEK 20 to 40m.

Procurement is a hypothesis, not a forecast.

  • Bravida has 50 to 60 central buyers and no unified purchasing system (former Caverion head of strategy, 18 January 2024). Implementation of a new central purchasing system is described as slow.
  • Peers' claimed procurement gains (up to 150bp, Morgan Stanley) need exactly the central control the Bravida Way avoids.
  • I include nothing in the base case. The upside, 0.2 to 0.5pp of gross margin over three years, would need a disclosed system, budget and timetable first.

What peers' programmes say about delivery.

  • Instalco's Q4 2024 programme: about 110 staff, 100 subcontractor contracts ended and eight loss-making subsidiaries closed, for SEK 65m of one-off cost and a SEK 25m goodwill write-down, aimed at about 35bp of margin.
  • Assemblin Caverion captured SEK 97m of annualised synergies by coordinating its Swedish units while keeping branch autonomy.
  • Bilfinger is cutting up to 1,500 staff to get back to 6% after its warning.
  • Pattern: decentralised installers get 30 to 40bp from structural simplification. Bravida's programme is in that range, and nothing suggests a second, larger one.

Regional catch-up, the larger lever (in the model's segment build).

  • Finland. From about 1.5% (FY2025) to about 3.3% (FY2026; DNB Carnegie: SEK 89m), 4.0% to 4.3% (FY2027) and about 4.5% (FY2028) on about SEK 2.5 to 2.8bn of core revenue. That is worth about SEK 60 to 75m by FY2028 against FY2025.
  • The new principal-contractor contract for atNorth sits in the same unit and is the risk to this path: about SEK 1.1bn of revenue in FY2027, roughly a 40% addition to the unit's core revenue, in a unit that wrote down projects in Q2 and Q3 2025.
  • Denmark. From 5.1% to about 5.5% to 5.8% as the turnaround matures and growth resumes (organic +11% in Q2 2026): worth about SEK 40 to 60m.
  • Norway. From about 6.2% to about 6.3% on the core, diluted by Green Mountain revenue at a cost-plus margin.

When it reaches the lines.

  • FY2026: savings of about SEK 40m, Finland +SEK 55m. Both are in my FY2026 adjusted EBITA of SEK 1,942m.
  • FY2027: savings +SEK 25m, Finland +SEK 24m.
  • FY2028: Finland +SEK 20m, plus about SEK 15m of further efficiency.
  • No step-change after FY2028. I see no evidence of a second programme. Management's record is one heroic regional intervention at a time.

Against consensus. Consensus's 7.0% to 7.1% for FY2027 to FY2028 would need efficiency and catch-up of about SEK 200 to 250m beyond what I can identify, or data-centre margins well above the group's. Cost elimination cannot close that gap.

P-04

After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

❌ D42

Judgement. Price will contribute about 2% to 3% a year to revenue from 2027, roughly the rate of cost inflation, and close to nothing to margin. Its margin effect is a timing effect.

  • When costs accelerate: a small, temporary squeeze. In 2027 I put it at about SEK 25m, about 7bp, because the Swedish wage round looks closer to 3% than to 8%.
  • When costs stabilise: a small recovery the following year.
  • On the data-centre contracts: price risk is better protected than the first set assumed, because the largest contract is cost-plus. The protection concentrates risk in atNorth.
  • The real pricing risk is cyclical, not inflationary. If the 2027 to 2028 rate rises stall the recovery, the more than 25,000 small firms in the market return to zero-margin bids. In this market prices fall when volume falls, with a two-to-four-quarter lag.

Revenue: what price adds.

  • Wages are about 35% to 45% of revenue (labour and subcontractors together are most of the 84% production cost):
  • Swedish agreements are expected just below 3.0% (Nordea) to 3.2% (Swedbank) a year;
  • Norway's 2026 agreement was 4.5%;
  • Danish jobs longer than 12 months are indexed.
  • Materials prices are rising again:
  • Rexel's non-cable selling prices turned positive at +1% to 2% in Q2 2026 after two years of deflation;
  • Systemair raised prices from June 2026 to protect a 10% margin floor;
  • Lindab intends to compensate fully for raw-material and transport costs.
  • Construction cost inflation: Sweden 3.7%, Norway 5.4% (September 2026).
  • So nominal pass-through of about 2.5% to 3.5% a year is needed to hold margins flat (ABG Sundal Collier: Nordic salaries rise 3% to 4% a year; flat margins require pricing in lockstep).
  • My core organic growth of +4.5% in FY2027 is about 2 points of volume and 2.5 points of price. In FY2028 to FY2030, about 1 point of volume and 2 points of price.

Margin: why price adds nothing in the core.

  • The ten-year EBITA margin envelope (5.2% to 6.9%) and the gross margin (14.2% to 15.2%) show no cumulative real pricing.
  • Swedish contracts (47% of sales) lack automatic indexation. Bravida pushes for index clauses and tells branches to allow for price increases in new contracts (2025 annual report), but the market sets price.
  • In standard installation the marginal price is set by the tail's utilisation:
  • Swedish tenders still draw up to 15 bidders against a historical five (DNB Carnegie, 8 September 2026);
  • smaller firms still bid at zero or negative margins (expert, March 2026);
  • new-build contractors have moved into renovation (Fasadgruppen).
  • One improving indicator: DNB Carnegie (28 September 2026) cites National Institute of Economic Research data showing a "significantly improved pricing environment" year on year in Sweden. That is consistent with the tail's idle capacity being absorbed, the balancing loop in P-05 working in Bravida's favour for now. I treat it as the reason the core margin does not erode in 2026 to 2027. I do not treat it as real price.

The 2027 lag, quantified.

  • About SEK 3 to 4bn of Swedish fixed-price installation backlog will be executed in 2027 at prices set in 2025 to 2026, labour about 35% of its cost.
  • If 2027 wage settlements run about 0.5 to 1 point above what branches priced, the squeeze is about SEK 10 to 30m. I model SEK 25m and recover SEK 15m in FY2028.
  • The tail case (agreements above 4%, or the 8% minimum-wage rise feeding into actual pay) is SEK 60 to 80m, about 20bp. This is the case the first set took as central.

The data-centre contracts.

  • Green Mountain (about SEK 4.3bn): cost-plus, with prepayments expected (DNB Carnegie, 18 June 2026). Copper, equipment and labour inflation pass through.
  • The cost of that protection is a margin that is probably mid-single-digit and capped. It is not the "highly margin accretive" outcome the bull case assumes.
  • atNorth (about SEK 2.2bn): principal contractor including civil works. The pricing mechanism is not disclosed, prepayments are expected, and the contract is two years long.
  • This is where fixed-price exposure to copper (forecast +38% in 2026) and equipment lead times (switchgear and transformers 128 to 160 weeks) could bite.
  • EcoDataCenter (SEK 650m to Q1 2028): Swedish, indexation not disclosed. Its size limits the risk.
  • Penalty clauses: a US data-centre engineering executive (17 February 2026) says delay penalties are rarely enforced because permitting and grid delays are shared. DNB Carnegie warns that liquidated damages could still erode margins. I carry about SEK 25m a year of risk allowance in FY2027 to FY2028 on top of a 6% contract margin.

Service (48% of sales).

  • Contracts run three to seven years with indexation clauses and warranty lock-in, and 91% of customers above SEK 5m renew.
  • This is where pass-through is cleanest. It is also where the price is lowest-risk and the margin about 100bp higher (Morgan Stanley).
  • The service mix is not rising, though. Installation has gained share (Sweden 51% to 53%, Denmark 51% to 55%), and the data-centre work is all installation.

What would change the judgement.

  • Upside:
  • Q3 and Q4 2026 Swedish segment margins above the prior year by more than the savings account for, which would mean real price;
  • disclosure that atNorth and EcoDataCenter carry escalation clauses.
  • Downside:
  • Swedish 2027 agreements above 4%;
  • a stalled order intake in core Sweden in H1 2027, which would restart price dumping in 2028.
P-05

After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

❌ D47

Judgement. Five loops are at work. On balance they make 2026 to 2027 better than the underlying business and 2028 to 2029 worse.

  • Front-loaded benefits. The data-centre reference loop, the absorption of competitors' idle capacity, and the cash advances on large contracts all reinforce now.
  • Delayed costs. Each has a delayed balancing response, and the responses arrive together in 2028 to 2029:
  • capacity caps the refill;
  • pricing turns if the recovery stalls;
  • cash advances are consumed.
  • The model consequence. Revenue growth of +11.6% in FY2027 falls to +2.4% in FY2028 and +0.4% in FY2029. Free cash flow is strong in FY2026, weak in FY2027 and normal afterwards. Margin progress slows after FY2027.
  • The most important insight is the capacity ceiling on the reference loop, below. It turns the FY2029 revenue air-pocket from a risk into something close to a structural feature of the current contract book.

Loop 1: the data-centre reference loop (reinforcing, with a capacity ceiling).

  • How it works.
  • Delivering data centres builds references and white-collar project depth: about 200MW since 2009, and EcoDataCenter's eighth project.
  • References win larger awards: Green Mountain, the largest order in company history, followed by atNorth.
  • Delivered sites generate follow-on service and retrofits worth about 1.5 times the original contract over time (the Meta site at Luleå, DNB Carnegie, 8 September 2026).
  • Evidence it is running. Three, possibly four, awards in June to August 2026 worth SEK 7 to 8bn.
  • The ceiling. A former chief executive of Bravida Sverige (13 July 2026) says the company can execute about two hyperscale-scale projects at once, given 40 to 60 white-collar managers per site and a central projects office of about 70.
  • Green Mountain (to early 2029) and atNorth (to about mid-2028) now fill that capacity.
  • So the loop cannot convert new large awards into revenue at scale until atNorth winds down in mid-2028. New wins in 2027 would mostly start in H2 2028 to 2029.
  • The model consequence. Data-centre revenue of SEK 3.3bn in FY2027 and SEK 2.6bn in FY2028 falls to about SEK 1.3bn in FY2029. That covers the Green Mountain tail plus a partial refill starting late 2028. FY2030 is about SEK 1.5bn.
  • What would loosen the ceiling.
  • Hiring or acquiring project managers. The Head of M&A could buy capability, not just density.
  • A joint-venture structure like DC Nordic, which shares the white-collar load.
  • Smaller co-location contracts (SEK 0.5 to 1bn) that the central office can run in parallel; the possible SEK 850m Swedish award fits this.
  • The competitive limits.
  • Assemblin Caverion set up a data-centre business area on 31 August 2026 and has the same white-collar depth. The next Nordic awards will be contested by two bidders, not one.
  • Owner-furnished equipment, used in 70% to 80% of large data-centre projects globally (expert, 21 October 2026), limits the installer's scope.
  • Net: a reinforcing loop for reputation and order intake, and a balancing one for revenue timing.

Loop 2: operating leverage on a pruned cost base (reinforcing now, self-limiting). This is P-01's mechanism: high incremental margin while slack exists, fading as it is used. It adds about 40bp in FY2026 and about 20bp in FY2027, and turns into the normal hiring-at-market-wage regime from late 2027.

Loop 3: competitors' idle capacity and price (balancing, currently favourable).

  • A recovering market fills small competitors' crews, which stops zero-margin bidding. DNB Carnegie's "significantly improved pricing environment" (28 September 2026) is the evidence.
  • The loop reverses if the recovery stalls. Deutsche Bank (24 October 2025) warned that a delayed recovery forces competitors to dump prices to cover overheads.
  • Timing: the Riksbank and Norges Bank tightening from late 2026 hits starts in 2027 and installation revenue in 2028 (revenue lags starts by several quarters). That is the same window as the data-centre roll-off.
  • In the model: about −SEK 20m of core pricing pressure in FY2028 and −SEK 15m in FY2029. The stalled-recovery downside (C-29) would be 30 to 50bp, not modelled in the base.

Loop 4: customer advances and cash (balancing, the "timing illusion").

  • Large contracts with prepayments lift cash conversion early and depress it as the advances are consumed. The company's cash conversion went from 112% (mid-2024) to 77% (Q2 2026) as an earlier advance unwound (CFO, May 2026), and Q3 2025 printed 63% on a build-up of contract assets.
  • Now Green Mountain and atNorth prepayments (H2 2026) plus Stavanger (about SEK 465m) make FY2026 strong.
  • In the model: working capital falls from 20.8% to 19.5% of revenue in FY2026, then returns to 20.0% in FY2027 as the advances are used on SEK 3.5bn of extra revenue. Free cash flow: SEK 1.52bn (FY2026, 105% of net income) then SEK 0.84bn (FY2027, 51%), recovering to about 93% to 100% in FY2028 to FY2029.
  • A refill with new advances would repeat the early inflow. A gap in awards exposes the outflow.
  • One unverified flag: an AlphaSense report cites an October 2026 call on "expanding non-recourse factoring". If confirmed, reported cash conversion would flatter underlying working capital, and the third set should check receivables sold.

Loop 5: share price, buybacks and acquisitions (reflexive, weakly balancing).

  • A higher share price buys fewer shares: SEK 115.85 for the first programme, SEK 141.79 for the second.
  • Private-equity consolidators (InstallatørGruppen paying 3.4x EBITA for bolt-ons and 5.5x for platforms; Nimlas targeting SEK 10bn to 40bn of revenue by 2031) bid up targets.
  • So acquisitions add less and buybacks retire fewer shares just when the company has the cash.
  • In the model: acquired growth of 1% to 2% a year and buybacks of about 1% to 1.3% of shares a year, about 1% of EPS growth from capital allocation.

What a stronger reinforcing case would look like. It would combine:

  • a new data-centre award in H1 2027 structured as a joint venture or smaller package, so that it does not hit the capacity ceiling;
  • Assemblin Caverion losing the next large Nordic tender;
  • the Swedish recovery broadening without rate damage.

Then FY2029 revenue would be flat to up and the margin path would hold about 6.8%. I put this at about one in four.

P-06

After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

❌ D40

Judgement. Weight and lean differ sharply by line and by horizon, and the reason is the same pattern each time.

  • Revenue in upswings: Bravida's record is to beat. Volume is visible (an order book of 1.34x installation sales plus about SEK 7bn of contracts), and the chief executive talks volume down.
  • Margin above 6.5%: the record is to miss. The target has been missed for more than ten years, and margin misses arrive 12 to 18 months into an upswing, when work priced earlier meets higher costs (2023 was the precedent).
  • Cash: the record is excellent on a ten-year average and unreliable in any single year.

The weighting I would give the forecasts:

Line Next 18 months (to end-2027) Months 18 to 48 (FY2028 to FY2030) Lean
Revenue High (contracted data-centre work about 10% of FY2027 revenue; core order book +21%) Low (refill and the rate cycle unknown) Slightly up for H2 2026 to H1 2027; down for FY2028 to FY2029
Core margin Medium for FY2026; medium-low for FY2027 Low Down against any path above 6.6%
Data-centre margin Low (no disclosure; first principal-contractor role at scale) Low Down for atNorth; neutral for Green Mountain (cost-plus)
Below-EBIT items and share count High High Neutral; dividend up against consensus
Free cash flow Medium for FY2026 (Stavanger, prepayments); low for FY2027 Medium on a multi-year average Up for FY2026, down for FY2027

The evidence behind each lean.

  1. Volume: lean up near term. - Q2 2026 organic growth was +9% against consensus of about +2%. Q1 revenue beat by 3%. The chief executive called it "too early to expect rapid growth" in May, before the +9%. - Comparisons stay easy through Q4 2026 (Q3 2025 organic −2.5%; Q4 2025 −2.2%). - DNB Carnegie expects about 12% organic growth in Q4 2026; I have about +13%.
  2. Margin: lean down beyond 6.5%. - The group target of above 7% has never been met. The peak was 6.5% (FY2021). The informal "reach or get close to 7% in FY2026", set in early 2025, has slipped. - In the last upswing (2022 to early 2023) Bravida met or beat on volume and held 6.5%. Misses followed in mid-to-late 2023 on contracts priced before inflation (Q2 2023 margin −30bp to 5.6%; Danish write-downs). - The present set-up mirrors that sequence. Contracts are being priced now, in 2026, ahead of the 2027 wage round and a copper rise.
  3. Turnarounds and specific programmes: give full weight. - "At or around 5%" in Denmark delivered 5.0% to 5.1%. - The Swedish merger came in on time and within its SEK 90 to 100m cost. - The ABEKA gain came in at SEK 158m against SEK 80m guided, though the cash was SEK 208m against SEK 300m. - So the Finnish recovery and the Swedish savings in my base case deserve high weight. A group-wide margin step does not, because the company has never delivered one.
  4. Large projects: low weight, lean down. - There is no record at this scale as principal contractor. - The relevant precedents are write-downs that arrived suddenly under percentage-of-completion accounting:
    • Bravida's own Denmark (2023 to 2024, segment margin to 0.1% to 1%) and Finland (Q3 2025, 0.6%);
    • Caverion EUR 59m, Eltel EUR 55m, Sweco's Finnish adjustments.
    • DNB Carnegie's downside on a problem contract is above SEK 400m.
    • I carry SEK 25m a year of allowance in FY2027 to FY2028. A single problem contract would be SEK 100 to 400m, a 20% to 30% chance over two years (first set, C-81), worth SEK 0.4 to 1.5 of EPS in the year it lands.
  5. Cash: lean up for FY2026, down for FY2027. - The ten-year company cash conversion is about 98%. - FY2025 was 79% and the last twelve months 77%. Q3 2025 printed 63% on a contract-asset build. - The near-term inflows (Stavanger, prepayments) are dated. The outflow, as advances are consumed, is mechanical.

How far to lean, in numbers.

  • Against consensus, my base case is:
  • FY2026: about 1% below on clean EPS (SEK 7.11 against 7.20); in line on revenue;
  • FY2027: 8% below on EPS (SEK 8.11 against 8.78) and 7% below on EBITA; in line on revenue;
  • FY2028: 10% below on EPS (SEK 8.51 against 9.45); 1% below on revenue.
  • The cone for FY2027 EPS is about SEK 7.0 to 9.0.
  • SEK 7.0: one problem contract of about SEK 250m, core organic growth of 2%, and incremental margin of 6%.
  • SEK 9.0: data-centre margin 7.5%, incremental margin held at 14%, benign wages.
  • Consensus at SEK 8.78 sits at about the 75th to 80th percentile of that cone. The skew against consensus is down. The skew against my base case is roughly balanced: the write-down risk is matched by the volume-surprise and distribution upside.

What would change the weights quickly.

  • The Q3 2026 print (23 October 2026) and the Q4 2026 print (February 2027). The tests:
  • core margin up more than about 40bp year on year;
  • Finland at or above 3.5%;
  • Swedish margin up by more than the savings explain;
  • an order book above SEK 22bn with core (non-data-centre) intake still growing.
  • If all four pass, raise the FY2027 margin to about 6.7% and close half the gap to consensus. If Finland or Sweden disappoint, lower the FY2027 margin to about 6.3%.
P-08

After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C58

Judgement. Below the operating line, the next four years will look cleaner and slightly more favourable to EPS than the last six.

  • Interest takes about 5% to 6% of EBITA, against 12% to 13% at the 2023 to 2024 rate peak. Tax holds at about 21%. There are no minorities, and associates are immaterial.
  • Buybacks shrink the share count by about 1% a year, against roughly flat to rising before 2026.
  • Net: net income grows a little faster than EBITA and EPS about 1 point a year faster than net income.
  • The two places where reported figures will diverge from the clean ones are both in FY2026. Reported EPS will be about SEK 0.5 to 1.0 above adjusted because of the tax-free ABEKA gain and, possibly, the Stavanger interest and cost award. Free cash flow will be volatile year to year (strong FY2026, weak FY2027) but average about 85% to 90% of net income, close to its FY2021 to FY2025 record.

Item by item.

  1. Net financial items: SEK −105m (FY2026), −120m (FY2027), −125m a year (FY2028 to FY2030). - History: about SEK −70 to −85m in FY2019 to FY2022 (FactSet gross interest; the net figure was likely lower); a peak in FY2023 to FY2024 as Swedish rates rose to 4%; about SEK −105 to −109m in FY2025 including about SEK 50m of lease interest. - The path is set by lease interest growing with the fleet, a Riksbank moving up from 1.75% and the FY2027 working-capital outflow. - Sensitivity is small: SEK 15m per percentage point.
  2. Tax: 21.0% on adjusted profit. The statutory rates are Sweden 20.6%, Norway and Denmark 22% and Finland 20%. The FY2025 reported rate was 20.8%, and DNB Carnegie models 20.9%. - The shift in mix toward Norway (Green Mountain) and Finland (atNorth) is broadly neutral. - FY2026 reported tax will be about 19.3% to 19.5%, because the SEK 158m ABEKA gain is tax-exempt (company, Q2 2026 report).
  3. One-off items. - FY2026 reported against adjusted:
    • ABEKA gain +SEK 158m (no tax; about +SEK 0.77 a share);
    • Swedish restructuring about −SEK 75m (−SEK 0.29 a share after tax);
    • possibly the Stavanger interest and cost award, about +SEK 145m (about +SEK 0.55 a share), if it was not accrued earlier and is booked once the ruling became final in September 2026.
    • Reported EPS is therefore about SEK 7.6 (without Stavanger) to SEK 8.1 (with it), against my adjusted SEK 7.11.
    • Brokers will treat these differently. FY2026 "consensus" will keep mixing bases until the full-year report (DNB Carnegie excludes; Deutsche Bank includes the restructuring; the July estimates from Oddo BHF and Nordea probably include the gain).
    • FY2027 to FY2030: no announced items.
    • The Danish arbitration (SEK 800m, 2028) is a binary outside the base case: a loss would be up to about SEK 3.1 a share one-off; a win, about SEK 800m of cash.
    • Credit-loss risk on data-centre customers is lower than the first set assumed (correction 4 in the preface), but the Northvolt precedent (about SEK 100m in 2024) shows the size of a single failure.
  4. Minority interests and associates: none material. Green Mountain is executed through the DC Nordic joint venture with Backe Industri, and the stated NOK 4.5bn is Bravida's share. Verify whether the joint venture is proportionally consolidated (its revenue in Bravida's revenue line) or equity-accounted (only profit, below EBITA). The first is assumed in my revenue path and by consensus's Norway forecast (+21% in FY2027). The second would remove about SEK 1.7 to 1.8bn of FY2027 to FY2028 revenue while leaving EPS broadly unchanged.
  5. Share count. - Basic shares outstanding were 204.6m at end-2025. - 2026 movements: +0.18m from incentive C-share conversions (May), −0.86m first buyback (May to July, SEK 115.85), −0.71m second buyback (to October, SEK 141.79), and about −0.3m from a third programme I assume for Q4 2026. - That gives about 203.0m at end-2026 and an average of about 204.1m. - Then SEK 400m (FY2027, including the Stavanger proceeds) and SEK 300m a year of buybacks at rising prices, less incentive conversions of 0.15 to 0.2m a year. - Year-end shares: about 200.6m (FY2027), 198.8m (FY2028), 197.2m (FY2029), 195.7m (FY2030). That is about −1.0% to −1.2% a year, against +0.2% a year in FY2019 to FY2025. - FactSet's diluted count (206.4m) is about 1% above basic. Brokers do not state their basis, so about 1% of any EPS comparison is definitional.
  6. Dividends. - Policy is at least 50% of net profit. Actual payout was 51% to 69% (63% for FY2025, SEK 3.80, SEK 777m). - I model about 59% to 60% of adjusted EPS: SEK 4.20, 4.80, 5.10, 5.25 and 5.60. - That is above FactSet consensus (SEK 4.00, 4.25, 4.40), which implies a falling payout and looks stale, and in line with DNB Carnegie (SEK 4.40, 5.30, 5.80) for FY2026 and below it after. - I keep extra distributions in buybacks rather than in an extraordinary dividend.

Against the last four to six years.

  • EPS. It grew 5.5% a year in FY2019 to FY2025, matching revenue, and was flat in FY2023 to FY2025. My path compounds at about 9% to 10% a year from FY2025 to FY2030 (from the brokers' SEK 5.88; about 9% from my clean SEK 6.1). About two-thirds of the five-year gain arrives in FY2026 to FY2027.
  • Contribution of below-EBIT items to EPS growth over FY2025 to FY2028:
  • share count about +0.2 of EPS (+2.4%);
  • interest about −0.08;
  • tax about nil.
  • Below-EBIT items add about 1 point a year. They are not a driver.
  • Free cash flow after lease principal (my estimates of the lease element for earlier years): about 134% (FY2019), 174% (FY2020), 83%, 81%, 71% (FY2021 to FY2023), 134% (FY2024), 70% (FY2025). The average was about 88% in FY2021 to FY2025.
  • The pattern is counter-cyclical: cash is strong when revenue falls (2020, 2024) and weak in growth years (2021 to 2023) and when advances unwind (2025).
  • My FY2026 to FY2030 average of about 86% repeats that pattern on a growth path with dated advances. FY2027 is the weak year.
  • Against consensus cash.
  • Consensus operating cash flow is SEK 2.51bn in FY2026 and 2.51bn in FY2027, against SEK 1.45bn in FY2025. Mine is SEK 2.10bn and 1.46bn.
  • For FY2027 the consensus figure needs working capital to fall while revenue rises by SEK 3.5bn and the advances are consumed. I see no mechanism for that short of new prepaid awards.
  • This is the largest single gap between my model and consensus in percentage terms, and the one most likely to surprise in FY2027.
P-09

After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

C52

Judgement. The next four years will be better than the last six, but in a front-loaded, step-then-plateau shape, not a compounding one.

  • FY2026 to FY2027 is a genuine step:
  • revenue +7.8% and +11.6%;
  • adjusted EBITA +15% and +13%;
  • adjusted EPS about +21% and +14%.
  • FY2028 to FY2029 is a plateau:
  • revenue +2.4% and +0.4% as the data-centre contracts complete and the rate-sensitive core slows;
  • EPS +5% and +3%.
  • The margin improves by about 70bp over five years (6.0% to 6.7%), against a flat ten-year envelope. That is real but modest, and it never reaches the 7% target in my forecast period.
  • Against consensus: my revenue is in line, EBITA 7% to 9% lower in FY2027 to FY2028, EPS 8% to 10% lower, dividends higher, and cash flow much lower in FY2027.

The net picture against history.

Measure FY2019 to FY2025 FY2022 to FY2025 My FY2025 to FY2028 My FY2025 to FY2030
Revenue growth a year 5.5% 2.4% 7.2% 5.4%
EBITA margin, start to end 6.0% to 6.0% (peak 6.5%) 6.4% to 6.0% 6.0% to 6.55% 6.0% to 6.67%
EPS growth a year (brokers' basis from FY2025) 5.5% (reported) about −1% (FY2022 to FY2025, reported basis) 13.1% 9.7%
Free cash flow after leases over net income, average about 107% about 88% (FY2021 to FY2025) about 83% about 86%
Share count change a year +0.2% +0.1% about −0.9% about −0.9%

What drives the EPS gain from FY2025 to FY2028 (from about SEK 6.1 on my clean FY2025 definition to SEK 8.51, +SEK 2.4):

  • Volume at a constant 6.0% margin: about +SEK 1.5 (about 60%).
  • Of this, the data-centre contracts contribute about SEK 0.4.
  • The core recovery, acquisitions and price pass-through contribute the rest.
  • Margin expansion of about 55bp: about +SEK 0.74 (about 30%).
  • Swedish savings, the Finnish recovery, recovery-phase incremental margin, and Danish and Norwegian edging up.
  • Partly offset by mix toward lower-margin data-centre and installation work.
  • Buybacks: about +SEK 0.2 (about 8%).
  • Interest: about −SEK 0.08.

The finding that matters most for the next Analyst.

  • The data-centre contracts carry most of the narrative weight in the stock: DNB Carnegie's "let's get structural", and the share price +68% in twelve months.
  • They carry about 8% of FY2027 EBITA (about SEK 173m of SEK 2,190m) and about a quarter of the FY2025 to FY2027 EPS increase.
  • About 70% to 75% of the earnings growth in my base case is the cyclical recovery of the core (volume, savings, Finland) on a business whose margin has never sustained above 6.5%. So predictability over the next 18 months rests mostly on the core recovery and the drop-through (the share of each extra krona of revenue that reaches EBITA), not on the data-centre contracts.
  • The data-centre contracts matter more to the downside (one write-down equals two to three quarters of the core's margin progress) and to FY2029 (the roll-off).

Line by line, net of everything above.

  • Revenue:
  • FY2026: +7.8%. Organic about +8%, including about 3 points from data-centre contracts; acquired about +1%; ABEKA −1.2%; currency about −0.3%.
  • FY2027: +11.6%. Core organic +4.5%; data-centre contracts about +6.7 points; acquired +1.5%; ABEKA −0.4%; currency −0.5%.
  • FY2028: +2.4%. Core +5% (organic 3%, acquired 2%); data-centre contracts −2 points.
  • FY2029: +0.4%. Core +4.5%; data-centre contracts −3.7 points.
  • FY2030: +5.4%.
  • Adjusted EBITA margin: 6.39%, 6.46%, 6.55%, 6.64%, 6.67%. The core business runs at 6.43% to 6.70%; the data-centre contracts at about 5.25% to 6%.
  • EBITDA margin (adjusted, EBITA plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles): 8.53%, 8.45%, 8.55%, 8.66%, 8.64%.
  • It dips in FY2027 even though EBITA margin rises, because the asset-light, subcontracted data-centre revenue carries little depreciation.
  • Consensus EBITDA margins (8.8%, 8.9%, 9.0%) are about 30 to 50bp higher on a broadly similar basis.
  • Net income: SEK 1,451m, 1,636m, 1,700m, 1,731m, 1,839m. Consensus net income for FY2026 to FY2028 is SEK 1,548m, 1,814m and 1,928m; the broker-sheet median FY2026 is SEK 1,468m.
  • Free cash flow: SEK 1.52bn, 0.84bn, 1.58bn, 1.74bn, 1.50bn.
  • Leverage including leases: about 0.8x to 1.2x throughout.

Effects of the other questions on the lines, in one place.

  • P-01 (operating leverage): keeps the margin path shallow after FY2026. It is the main source of the gap to consensus.
  • P-02 (financial leverage): neutral to EPS; a large unused option for distributions.
  • P-03 (cost elimination): worth about 13bp (FY2026) and 7bp (FY2027) from Swedish savings, plus regional catch-up of about 40 to 50bp by FY2028.
  • P-04 (pricing): revenue +2% to 3% a year from price; margin effect about −7bp in FY2027 and +5bp in FY2028.
  • P-05 (feedback loops): front-load revenue and cash. They create the FY2028 to FY2029 plateau and the FY2027 cash trough.
  • P-06 (track record): lean the margin and FY2027 cash down, near-term revenue slightly up.
  • P-08 (below the operating line): about +1 point a year of EPS from buybacks; reported FY2026 EPS above adjusted.
P-10

Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

C50

Judgement. My base case is a cyclical-recovery-plus-contracted-projects case.

  • Revenue: in line with consensus through FY2027 (SEK 30.4bn and 33.9bn), then a plateau (SEK 34.7bn and 34.9bn in FY2028 to FY2029) as the data-centre contracts complete. The completions are faster than the company's project capacity allows it to refill.
  • Margin: rises from 6.0% (FY2025) to 6.4% (FY2026) on the recovery, savings and Finland, then only about 10bp a year to 6.7% by FY2030. The 7% target is not reached in the forecast period.
  • Adjusted EPS: SEK 7.11, 8.11, 8.51, 8.74 and 9.36. That is about 1% below consensus in FY2026, 8% below in FY2027 and 10% below in FY2028.
  • Free cash flow: strong in FY2026 (Stavanger and prepayments), weak in FY2027 (advances consumed), normal thereafter.
  • Distributions: dividends above consensus (about 60% payout) plus SEK 300 to 400m a year of buybacks, with a large unused balance-sheet option.

The confidence ranking of the base case:

  1. Revenue to FY2027: high.
  2. Below-EBIT items: high.
  3. FY2026 margin: medium-high.
  4. FY2027 margin: medium.
  5. FY2027 cash: medium-low.
  6. FY2028 to FY2030 revenue: low (refill and rates).

The range around the base case for FY2027 (the year the third set should test hardest).

Case Probability (my judgement) Revenue EBITA margin EPS What has to happen
Downside about 20% about 33.0bn about 5.8% to 6.0% about 7.0 One problem data-centre contract (about SEK 250m), most likely atNorth; core organic about 2% as rate rises bite early; drop-through about 6%
Base about 60% 33.9bn 6.46% 8.11 As above
Upside about 20% about 34.5bn about 7.0% about 9.0 Data-centre margin about 7.5%; drop-through held at about 14%; wage agreements about 3%; a further award without slippage
  • The probability-weighted EPS is about SEK 8.1. Consensus at SEK 8.78 needs close to the upside case.

Where my base case most differs from consensus, and the logic.

  1. Margin persistence. Consensus extrapolates the recovery-phase drop-through (about 11% to 12%) to the whole FY2025 to FY2027 increase and puts data-centre work at or above the group margin. I fade drop-through as the slack is used and hold the data-centre contracts at about 5% to 6%. This is 80% to 90% of the EPS gap.
  2. Net financial items. Consensus's pre-tax line implies about SEK 62 to 67m a year; I use SEK 105 to 125m. That is about SEK 0.15 to 0.25 of EPS.
  3. Cash. Consensus operating cash flow of SEK 2.5bn a year in FY2026 to FY2027 compares with my SEK 2.1bn and 1.5bn.
  4. Dividend. Mine is higher, because consensus implies a falling payout against a policy of at least 50% and actual payouts of 51% to 69%.
  5. FY2029. My EPS of SEK 8.74 is in line with the single broker's SEK 8.80 by a different route: my revenue is higher (SEK 34.9bn against 33.4bn, because of core growth and a partial refill), while my margin is lower (6.6% against about 7.0%).

The hinges the third set should test, because these are where the predictability of the next 18 months is decided:

  1. Does core drop-through stay above about 10% into 2027, or fade as I assume? Watch the Swedish and Danish segment margins year on year in Q3 2026 to Q2 2027.
  2. Does atNorth deliver at about 5% or better as principal contractor in Finland? Watch the Finnish segment margin with atNorth inside it, and any provisions.
  3. Does working capital reverse in 2027? Watch contract liabilities (advances) and contract assets, and whether non-recourse factoring is being used to smooth reported cash conversion.
  4. Can the company refill the data-centre book while two large projects run? Watch award announcements, joint-venture structures and project-manager hiring.
  5. Which basis is each broker on? The ABEKA gain, the restructuring and the Stavanger interest award will keep FY2026 consensus noisy until February 2027.

Part 4. The reasoning behind the inputs

Bases used in Parts 1 to 3.

  • EBITDA is adjusted: EBITA before items affecting comparability, plus depreciation and amortisation excluding acquired intangibles (including right-of-use depreciation under IFRS 16). Unrounded margins: 8.53%, 8.45%, 8.55%, 8.66%, 8.64%. Excluded items:
  • the SEK 158m tax-free ABEKA gain (Q2 2026);
  • Swedish restructuring of about SEK 75m in FY2026 (SEK 20m Q1, SEK 40m Q2, about SEK 15m Q3);
  • any booking in H2 2026 of the about SEK 145m interest and cost element of the Stavanger award.
  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles is close to nil for Bravida on the FY2025 profit and loss arithmetic, so the model's EBIT equals adjusted EBITA.
  • Net financial items include lease interest (about SEK 50 to 60m). Lease payments are principal repayments only.
  • Working capital is on FactSet's basis (receivables plus inventories less payables): 20.8% at end-FY2025 and 22.5% at Q2 2026. I use it as the proxy for total operating working capital, including customer advances.
  • Acquisitions less disposals in FY2026 is SEK +208m from ABEKA less about SEK 190m of bolt-on spend (SEK 113m in H1 per FactSet, plus Åslunds El and others).
  • Buybacks in FY2026: the two completed SEK 100m programmes plus about SEK 50m of an assumed third programme in Q4 2026. FY2027 includes the redistribution of the Stavanger cash.
  • Change in share count is conversions of incentive C shares into ordinary shares (178,138 in May 2026).

Segments: revenue and margin by segment, where the company reports them

The company reports four country segments (Sweden, Denmark, Norway, Finland) with EBITA, plus an installation and service split. Full-year FY2025 segment EBITA is not fully in the evidence (Denmark's SEK 370m is), so the FY2025 column is reconstructed and should be checked against the segment note.

Cross-cutting split.

  • Core business (excluding data-centre contracts): EBITA margin 6.43% (FY2026), 6.59%, 6.68%, 6.66%, 6.70% (FY2030).
  • Data-centre contract revenue of about SEK 1.26bn, 3.3bn, 2.6bn, 1.3bn and 1.5bn, at about 5.0% to 6.0% EBITA. FY2027 to FY2028 carry a SEK 25m a year risk allowance.
  • Service is about 48% of sales with about 100bp higher margin than installation. The mix shifts toward installation while the data-centre contracts run.

The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue.

  • Core organic growth: about +5% (FY2026), +4.5%, +3.0%, +2.5%, +3.0%.
  • FY2026 is the lagged Swedish and Nordic recovery on easy comparisons (Q3 2025 organic −2.5%, Q4 2025 −2.2%).
  • From 2027 roughly half is price that passes through wage and material inflation.
  • FY2028 to FY2029 slow as the Riksbank and Norges Bank tightening from late 2026 reaches installation revenue.
  • Acquired growth: +1.0%, +1.5%, +2.0%, +2.0%, +2.0%. Private-equity consolidators bid for the same targets.
  • ABEKA: −1.25 points (FY2026) and −0.4 points (FY2027).
  • Currency: about −0.3% and −0.5% in FY2026 to FY2027.
  • Data-centre contracts, by year (FY2026 / FY2027 / FY2028 / FY2029):
  • Green Mountain (Bravida's share NOK 4.5bn, cost-plus, end-2026 to early 2029): 100 / 1,700 / 1,750 / 650.
  • atNorth (about SEK 2.2bn, principal contractor including civil works, about two years; about SEK 300m allowed for slippage): 300 / 1,050 / 550.
  • EcoDataCenter (SEK 650m to Q1 2028): 230 / 350 / 50.
  • A possible further SEK 850m Swedish award (press release 25 August 2026, unverified, half weight): 50 / 200 / 100.
  • Refill limited until about mid-2028: management can run about two large projects at once.

Margin.

  • Recovery-phase drop-through (the share of each extra krona of revenue reaching EBITA) of about 13% in H2 2026, fading toward the average margin as idle capacity is used in 2027. About 90% of the cost base is variable.
  • Swedish savings: SEK 40m inside FY2026, the full SEK 65m from FY2027.
  • Finland from about 1.5% (FY2025) to 3.3%, 4.0% and 4.5%.
  • 2027 Swedish wage round: a −SEK 25m lag effect, recovered by SEK 15m in FY2028. Agreements of about 3.0% to 3.2% are expected; SEB's 8% is the minimum-wage rise.
  • Core pricing pressure of −SEK 20m (FY2028) and −SEK 15m (FY2029) as the cycle slows.

Other lines.

  • Depreciation and amortisation grows with the vehicle fleet and premises (about SEK 633m in FY2025; about SEK 162 to 163m a quarter in H1 2026).
  • Net financial items:
  • FY2025 was about SEK −105 to −109m including about SEK −50m of lease interest. FactSet's SEK −181m is gross interest expense.
  • The cost rises modestly with the Riksbank (1.75% now, hikes signalled) and the FY2027 working-capital outflow.
  • The company's sensitivity is SEK 15m per percentage point.
  • Tax: 21% blended (Sweden 20.6%; Norway and Denmark 22%; Finland 20%). The FY2025 reported rate was 20.8%. FY2026 reported will be about 19.4% because the ABEKA gain is tax-exempt.
  • Working capital:
  • FY2026 falls to 19.5% on the Stavanger receipt (about SEK 320m principal, plus SEK 145m interest and costs) and the data-centre prepayments expected in H2 2026.
  • FY2027 rises to 20.0% as the advances are consumed on SEK 3.5bn of extra revenue, an outflow of about SEK 860m.
  • Neutral thereafter.
  • Dividend: about 59% to 60% of adjusted EPS. The policy is at least 50%; the actual was 63% for FY2025.
  • Buybacks: SEK 300 to 400m a year. Bank net debt stays below 0.8x EBITDA excluding leases, so far larger distributions are possible.

Bravida gives no short-term numerical guidance. Its medium-term targets are unchanged and undated.
